### cmd/harness

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The command is a set of cobra verbs over internal/harness packages. The newer subsonic verbs (record-oracle, grade) are careful and idiomatic, especially the reference resolver. The package costs itself most on consistency across verbs: each verb solves the same problems differently (test seams, HTTP clients, flag validation, default paths, shared helpers parked in whichever file came first), and the code copies helpers from internal/harness/oracle with comments that defend the copy. An agent adding a tenth verb has no single pattern to follow.

13 findings: 2 high, 7 medium, 4 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `cmd/harness/freeze.go:104` | high | (P6) repoRoot is a byte-for-byte copy of internal/harness/oracle/oracle.go:81. missFileName (capture.go:45) copies the constant at oracle.go:40. resolveVenv (freeze.go:88) and oracleVenvEnvVar (freeze.go:18) mirror oracle's test-only venv lookup. The comments present each copy as a decision: "matching internal/harness/oracle's own helper", "agree on it through the file format ..., not a shared symbol". So the code teaches agents to copy a helper and write down that they did. Production code that copies a test helper is a boundary in the wrong place. | Keep one implementation in the package that owns the contract. oracle should export the miss-file name and the default-venv resolution, since capture and freeze are their production callers under record 0003, for example `const MissFile = "misses.json"` and `func DefaultVenv() (string, error)`. A single repo-root finder can live in a small internal package that both oracle and cmd/harness call. Then delete the copies and the comments that defend them. |
| `cmd/harness/capture.go:76` | medium | Flag validation follows two idioms. oracleup.go:42 and recordoracle.go:136 use cobra's MarkFlagsMutuallyExclusive. capture hand-writes the same check plus a one-of check in a switch, seed-items hand-writes `--facts` against `--overrides` (seeditems.go:40), and synth, freeze, and seed-items hand-write `--x is required`. Only grade, oracle-up, and record-oracle set `Args: cobra.NoArgs`, so `harness freeze foo` silently ignores foo. All nine verbs repeat `SilenceUsage: true, SilenceErrors: true` even though the root already sets both. The repeats only matter when a test executes a subcommand on its own. | Declare the constraints through cobra in every verb: `cmd.MarkFlagRequired("case")`, `cmd.MarkFlagsMutuallyExclusive("mbid", "case")`, `cmd.MarkFlagsOneRequired("mbid", "case")`, `cmd.MarkFlagsRequiredTogether("mbid", "out")`, and `Args: cobra.NoArgs` everywhere. Set the Silence flags once on the root, and have tests run verbs through newRootCmd() with SetArgs. |
| `cmd/harness/capture.go:151` | medium | The block that paces, fetches, writes the response, and appends a cassette.Request appears twice in runCapture (151-162 and 165-175) and a third time in the runCaptureMisses loop (223-234). releaseRequest (294-299) is a one-line wrapper around cassette.ReleaseLookup whose doc comment restates the callee, and TestReleaseRequestIsCanonical tests cassette's encoding from the wrong package. synth.go:73-78 writes responses/001.json with its own MkdirAll and os.WriteFile when writeResponse already does that. | Add one method, `func (c *capturer) fetch(ctx context.Context, method, pathQuery string) (cassette.Request, error)`, that holds the pacer, user agent, directory, and next number. runCapture calls it once or twice and runCaptureMisses calls it per miss. Call cassette.ReleaseLookup directly and move its test to cassette. synth calls writeResponse(outDir, 1, doc). |
| `cmd/harness/fetchxsd.go:37` | medium | runFetchXSD and runFetchUnidecode (fetchunidecode.go:40) are one body written twice: resolve the cache path, return early if the cache verifies, fetch, MkdirAll, atomic write, print the same message with a different noun. Each also has its own 30s timeout constant whose comment just quotes a byte count (fetchunidecode.go:20, fetchxsd.go:19). The two differ only because xmlrule offers Cached() and unidecode does not, so the harness rebuilds that check itself as wheelChecksumMatches (fetchunidecode.go:65). | Give unidecode a `CachedWheel() ([]byte, error)` that matches xmlrule.Cached, then write one helper in one fetch.go: `func fillCache(ctx context.Context, stderr io.Writer, noun string, path func() (string, error), cached func() ([]byte, error), fetch func(context.Context, *http.Client) ([]byte, error)) error`. Both RunE closures call it. Delete wheelChecksumMatches and the second timeout constant. |
| `cmd/harness/oracleup.go:47` | medium | runOracleUp takes a *cobra.Command only to reach OutOrStdout and ErrOrStderr, which ties the logic to cobra. Every other runX takes io.Writer (runGrade's status, runFetchXSD's stderr). Inside the same verb, the flag slice is `collections` in newOracleUpCmd (line 26) and `collectionFlags` in runOracleUp and in the other two verbs. | Use `func runOracleUp(ctx context.Context, stdout, stderr io.Writer, manifest string, collectionFlags []string, digests string) error`, or reuse recordConfig's shape. Name the flag slice collectionFlags everywhere. |
| `cmd/harness/recordoracle.go:118` | medium | Default paths are resolved three ways. grade calls defaultSubsonicPaths unconditionally (grade.go:37). record-oracle calls it inside resolveCollections and then a second time in RunE behind `if spec == "" \|\| out == ""`. resolveCollections (oracleup.go:106) guards its call with `digests == "" \|\| (len(cols) == 0 && manifest == "")`. Those conditions exist only to skip a cheap go.mod walk, and they make it hard to see which default applies when. | In each RunE, resolve the paths once with `p, err := defaultSubsonicPaths()`, fill every flag with `cmp.Or(flag, p.field)`, and pass resolved values down. resolveCollections then reduces to `parseCollections` plus a one-collection fallback and drops its digests parameter and return value. |
| `cmd/harness/seeditems.go:110` | medium | runSeedItemsFacts repeats the first three steps of runSeedItems (seeditems.go:74-85 against 111-122): LoadCase, refuse an empty request log, read the first response. The facts path can also lose its five-line comment explaining why it never calls Write, because its body will contain no write. |
| `cmd/harness/main.go:7` | low | (P5) The package doc lists each verb with its flags, which repeats every cobra Short and flag help string by hand, and it has already drifted: oracle-up and record-oracle are listed without their --manifest, --collection, --digests, --spec, and --out flags. It also coins "the cassette substrate" (line 3), the kind of reader-must-learn term record 0006 steers away from. | Keep the doc to what the tool is and the contact-env rule, and leave per-verb usage to `harness help`, as cmd/covdata does in its short doc. Or generate the list from the command tree. Replace "cassette substrate" with a plain phrase. |
| `cmd/harness/recordoracle.go:315` | low | (P7) `at := fmt.Errorf(...)` builds a fresh error on every request just to serve as a prefix, and each use wraps it with `%w: %w`. That puts a meaningless non-sentinel error into every chain, and an agent will copy it as the way to add context. | Use `at := fmt.Sprintf("%s: request %d (%s)", ...)` and `fmt.Errorf("%s: %w", at, err)`, so the only wrapped errors are real causes and sentinels. |
| `cmd/harness/recordoracle.go:533` | low | Small naming drift. `one` is too terse for a package-level function. The sentinels mix shapes (errSpec, errBadRef, errOutExists, errNoVersion). newFetchXSDCmd and runFetchXSD drop the 'subsonic' in the verb name fetch-subsonic-xsd, unlike newSeedItemsCmd and newOracleUpCmd, which match their verbs exactly. runSeedItemsFacts reads awkwardly. os.IsNotExist (capture.go:240, freeze.go:70) sits next to errors.Is(err, os.ErrNotExist) (capture.go:193, 252) in the same files. | Rename one to soleID, errSpec to errBadSpec, the XSD pair to newFetchSubsonicXSDCmd and runFetchSubsonicXSD, and runSeedItemsFacts to runFacts or printFacts. Use errors.Is(err, fs.ErrNotExist) throughout. |

### internal/atomicfile

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. This is a small, correct atomic-write primitive. WriteStream is the one implementation and WriteFile is a three-line adapter over it, and both exports have several production callers (cmd/harness, internal/auth, internal/store, internal/engine/tagio, internal/engine/matcher/unidecode, internal/harness/subsonic). What costs it most is one ordering slip: the chmod runs after the fsync, so the durability claim the doc makes does not cover the file's mode. The docs are also arranged backwards, with the full mechanism on the wrapper and a "same way" pointer on the implementation.

5 findings: 0 high, 1 medium, 4 low. Held by patterns: P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/atomicfile/atomicfile.go:54` | medium | The mode changes after the data is synced. The order is tmp.Sync (47), then tmp.Close (51), then os.Chmod(tmpPath, perm) by path (54). Nothing fsyncs the chmod, which is an inode metadata change, so the doc's power-cut claim (lines 31-33) does not cover the mode the doc says callers depend on (lines 27-30). The chmod also goes back through the path after the handle is closed, when every other step works on the open file. An experienced reviewer would read this as an ordering slip in the one place the package exists to get right. | Set the mode on the open handle before syncing, the way google/renameio does: `if err := tmp.Chmod(perm); err != nil { _ = tmp.Close(); return fmt.Errorf("chmod %s: %w", tmpPath, err) }` placed right after the write callback and before `tmp.Sync()`. Then drop the path-based os.Chmod. |
| `internal/atomicfile/atomicfile.go:67` | low | (P7) The error verbs disagree for the same operation. The file sync wraps as "sync %s" (line 49) and the directory sync as "fsync %s" (line 67). Both are (*os.File).Sync. Identifier naming is otherwise clean: WriteFile mirrors os.WriteFile's name and argument order, the callback comes last, and tmpPath and dirFile read plainly. atomicfile.WriteFile follows os.WriteFile and renameio.WriteFile, so it is not a stutter worth a finding. | Use one verb for both: `fmt.Errorf("sync %s: %w", dir, err)`. |
| `internal/atomicfile/atomicfile_test.go:299` | low | (P9) The tests restate each other. WriteFile is a one-line adapter over WriteStream, yet TestWriteFile (12-73) and the TestWriteStream table (196-297) cover the same rows (creates, overwrites, perm, empty). TestWriteStreamCreatesTempFileInTargetDir (304-338) copies TestWriteFileCreatesTempFileInTargetDir (97-131) line for line. writeAll (175-180) re-creates WriteFile's closure. No seams or unfailable fakes, and the expectations are independent of the code under test. | Test the mechanism once, through WriteStream's table plus its TMPDIR test. Keep one WriteFile case to show the adapter passes data and perm through, and delete the mirrored TMPDIR test. |

### internal/auth

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The security mechanism is careful and often excellent: canonical credential encoding, the MAC checked before any field is read, first-writer-wins nonces that carry their own lifetime, and an eviction policy that never resets a spent bucket. Around that core the package exports most of its internals, keeps a subscriber hook and an interface method nothing calls, has two gates and two epoch checks that copy each other, and runs at a comment density of 0.63 against net/http's 0.46, much of it citations of the review process.

20 findings: 4 high, 9 medium, 7 low. Held by patterns: P1 (4), P2 (2), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/auth/request.go:31` | high | CheckRequest (request.go:31-58) repeats RequireAdmin's body (admin.go:42-67): the clock read, the Sec-Fetch-Site test, VerifyIdentity, the reason/level switch through gateRefusalReason, the log line, and the refreshed-cookie write. The two differ only in the !v.Admin case and the log message. A third gate, such as a bearer mount, would be written as a third copy. | Make one unexported core the implementation and have both gates call it: func verify(cfg GateConfig, r *http.Request) (v VerifiedIdentity, now time.Time, reason string, level slog.Level, err error). It returns before any cookie is set. RequireAdmin adds the admin case and then writes the cookie, and CheckRequest maps the level to its two error shapes. Both keep the order of setting the cookie only after their own check. |
| `internal/auth/admin.go:12` | medium | AdminConfig configures CheckRequest, which admits any signed-in member, and the cmd verifiers for the API and tus mounts take it (cmd/dubplate-server/api.go:32, intake.go:73). The name says admin-only, so a reader of tusVerifier(cfg auth.AdminConfig) assumes an admin gate that does not exist. | Name the struct for what it configures, for example GateConfig or SessionCheckConfig, with RequireAdmin and CheckRequest both taking it. |
| `internal/auth/epoch.go:251` | medium | CheckLink (epoch.go:251-267) is CheckSession (epoch.go:225-241) with a different time field and a different sentinel. Both run the same normalize-read-compare sequence. | Keep one implementation, `func (e *EpochStore) revokedAt(address string, t time.Time) (bool, error)`, and have checkSession return errSessionRevoked and checkLink return errLinkRevoked when it reports true. |
| `internal/auth/session.go:218` | medium | VerifySession composes authenticateSession and checkLife, and MagicLinkSeam.VerifyIdentity composes the same two calls with a setting read between them (seam.go:112-122). Production uses only the seam's version, and deadcode reports VerifySession as unreachable. The package therefore keeps two compositions of the same check, and one of them is used only by tests. | Delete VerifySession from session.go. Put its composition in a session_test.go helper, and have serve_test.go:1934 present the refreshed cookie on a second request instead of decoding it. |
| `internal/auth/store.go:46` | medium | The type is called Store, but every comment and local variable calls it the leaf: leafDirName, 'the auth leaf', 'Open creates the leaf', and `leaf, err := auth.Open(...)` in sentinel_test.go:30 and member_test.go:552. The package also imports internal/store, whose Handle is the real store, so roster.go and reads.go have two unrelated 'stores' in scope. NonceStore.store and the OpenNonceStore parameter named store add a third use of the word. Also, auth.Open does not say what it opens. | Rename to `type Leaf`, `OpenLeaf(stateDir string) (*Leaf, error)` and `LeafDir`, which matches the code's own vocabulary. While there, rename OpenEpochStore, which opens nothing, to NewEpochStore. |
| `internal/auth/config.go:498` | low | The doc comments at config.go:498, 507 and 516 say 'an [Setting]'. EpochPath (store.go:87) has no doc comment of its own and shares NoncePath's. LinkURL refuses an empty prefix only 'for a future caller' (token.go:159-168), which is defensive code for a caller that does not exist. | Change to 'a [Setting]' and give each exported method its own doc line. Drop the LinkURL prefix guard, since serve.NewPrefix already holds that invariant. |
| `internal/auth/handlers.go:207` | low | The exported Handlers and the unexported handlers differ only in case, so a reader has to track which one a method hangs on. | Rename the unexported type to something like `endpoints` or `authServer`. |
| `internal/auth/handlers.go:528` | low | writeAnswer calls writeJSON, which calls writeJSONHeader, and each of the three has exactly one caller. The chain is two extra layers over the same four lines. | Collapse the chain into `func writeAnswer(w http.ResponseWriter, status int, a answer)` with the header sets inline. |
| `internal/auth/nonce.go:355` | low | syncDir repeats the directory fsync that atomicfile.WriteStream already performs (internal/atomicfile/atomicfile.go:61-68). | Export one SyncDir from internal/atomicfile, which would then have two callers, and call it here. |
| `internal/auth/roster.go:239` | low | normalizeMemberAddress is a one-line pass-through, and its comment says it is the roster's single resolution point. The claim is false: identity.NormalizeAddress is called directly at epoch.go:102,174,226,252, handlers.go:290, token.go:113 and session.go:108. A comment that contradicts the code teaches the wrong invariant. | Delete the wrapper and call identity.NormalizeAddress, and point Member.Address's doc (roster.go:117) at it. |
| `internal/auth/roster.go:381` | low | AddMember, RemoveMember and DemoteMember take a *slog.Logger on every call, and revoke passes it down. Every other component here holds its logger in its config (AdminConfig.Logger, HandlersConfig.Logger). The two styles split within one package. | Give Roster a logger field set by NewRoster, and drop the per-call parameter. |
| `internal/auth/roster.go:489` | low | The same raw-bytes-to-UserID length check is written three times: reads.go:30-34, roster.go:425-428 and roster.go:489-491. | Add one helper, `func userIDFrom(raw []byte) (identity.UserID, error)`, and have all three sites call it. |

### internal/backup

Proud: no. Verdict: Sound with nits. The machinery is excellent: classify's cancel-first precedence, the bounded lastLine writer, the prune safety check, and the restore steps that resume from a record. The exported surface is where it falls short: ParseRemote and Remote are exported with no outside caller, the doctor probe takes an options struct holding a timeout that record 0014 says belongs on the context, and doctor.go rebuilds the package's own rclone runner and remote-name rule beside the originals.

15 findings: 2 high, 7 medium, 6 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (5), P3 (2), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/backup/doctor.go:97` | medium | firstInvalidRemoteNameRune restates the alphabet validName (remote.go:41) already checks. remoteParseError (doctor.go:80) then works out again why ParseRemote refused the value, with a fallback that its own comment at doctor.go:88 calls unreachable. doctor.go:49 discards the parsed Remote and cuts the name out with strings.Cut a second time. One rule now lives in three places. | Keep one predicate, `func isNameRune(r rune) bool`, and in parseRemote use `if i := strings.IndexFunc(name, func(r rune) bool { return !isNameRune(r) }); name == "" \|\| i >= 0`, with an error message that names the offending rune. Doctor then returns "invalid remote: " + err.Error() and reads the name from the parsed value. remoteParseError, firstInvalidRemoteNameRune, and the unreachable branch all go. |
| `internal/backup/doctor.go:149` | medium | featuresStatus builds its own exec.CommandContext with its own Cancel, its own WaitDelay constant (featuresWaitDelay, doctor.go:20, next to waitDelay at rclone.go:22), and unbounded stderr capture read back by lastNonBlankLine (doctor.go:183). transport.run (rclone.go:59) already does all of this, and its lastLine writer exists precisely so stderr stays bounded. This is a second rclone runner with different policies, and the next probe will copy whichever one it finds first. | `var stdout bytes.Buffer; err := transport{bin: bin}.run(ctx, &stdout, "backend", "features", name+":", "--low-level-retries", "1")`, then `if ctx.Err() != nil` returns 'not checked', and `if f, ok := errors.AsType[*failure](err)` returns f.message. Delete featuresWaitDelay and lastNonBlankLine. |
| `internal/backup/restore.go:505` | medium | One package has two different things called checkLibrary: the function at guard.go:50 (the local short-tree guard for a backup) and the method source.checkLibrary (the remote library and free-space check for a restore). In the same way, the package-level function list (reconcile.go:200) writes a report section while transport.list (rclone.go:114) runs lsjson, and restorer.transport() (restore.go:213) has the same name as the type it returns. A reader has to resolve each name by its receiver. | Use names that say which side they check: guardLibrary or checkLocalTree for guard.go:50, source.checkRemoteLibrary for restore.go:505, writeSection for reconcile.go:200, and restorer.newTransport (or a package-level newTransport(inherit)) for restore.go:213. |
| `internal/backup/guard.go:92` | low | Failure codes, which are a contract stored in job records and matched by ops/local and cmd tests, appear as 17 raw string literals passed to fail(). rclone.go:123 matches "rclone-exit-3" by string. A typo silently changes the contract, and nothing lists the set of codes. | Declare a const block of codes in one place, for example `const (codeLibraryMissing = "library-missing"; codeLibraryShort = "library-short"; ...)`, next to the failure type, and use the constants at every fail() site and at the exit-3 check. |
| `internal/backup/restore.go:25` | low | Several step constant names do not match their values: stepSetAside = "swap-out", stepLibraryOwed = "library", stepReconcileOwed = "reconcile", stepOpened = "open". Most names are past participles, but two end in Owed. Elsewhere, prune.pruneSnapshots and prune.pruneArchive (prune.go:102, 131) stutter against their receiver, report.unfiled and report.unlisted (reconcile.go:40-41) are coined words where the rendered text says 'tracks without a file' and 'files without a track', and RestoreConfig.check (restore.go:105) does the same job as Job.validate under a different name. | Make each value match its constant name (stepSetAside = "set-aside"), and use p.snapshots and p.archive, missingFiles and untrackedFiles (or tracksWithoutFile and filesWithoutTrack), and validate for both configs. |
| `internal/backup/restore.go:411` | low | The block that folds a failure code into the message text, `if f, ok := errors.AsType[*failure](err); ok { err = fmt.Errorf("%s: %w", f.code, err) }`, appears at both restore.go:411 and fetch.go:114. Building a transport (LookPath for rclone, then transport{bin, inherit}) is also written twice, at backup.go:114/125 and restore.go:213-219, and LookPath appears a third time at doctor.go:64. The full-report bytes `append([]byte(head), rep.render(math.MaxInt)...)` are built at both reconcile.go:115 and restore.go:632. | `func newTransport(inherit func() ([]*os.File, error)) (transport, error)` as the single LookPath site. `func codeErr(err error) error` for the fold. reportOf returns the rendered full report, or add `func fullReport(head string, r *report) []byte`. |

### internal/config

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. This package holds the only DUBPLATE_* namespace wall and the declaration registry, and the mechanism is sound: KeyError leaves are joined into one error, refusals print in sorted order, registration panics follow the flag/sql/expvar precedent, and setting validators are compiled at registration. It falls short of the bar on duplication. Each of the seven declaration families repeats its own membership, collision, and bounds code even though a key-to-family claims map already exists. It has two identical key structs and two bounds checkers with different rules, and its comments run at 1.6 times the density of flag, mostly as inline rationale and rebuttals.

17 findings: 4 high, 8 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P2 (2), P3 (1), P4 (4).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/config/keys.go:59` | high | (P5) WallRow (keys.go:59-63) is field for field the same struct as KeyDecl (keys.go:42-46): Name, Description, Mark. Threshold and Setting carry the same three facts but call the name field Key (thresholds.go:88, settings.go:60). Because there are four shapes, requireDescribed takes four loose arguments (registry.go:128), and every caller that walks keys has to unpack each family by hand. An agent adding a family will copy a fifth shape. | Use one key declaration for all of them. Drop WallRow, have AddWallRow take a KeyDecl (or keep `type WallRow = KeyDecl` only if the name carries meaning), and embed it: `type Threshold struct { KeyDecl; Kind Kind; Default, Doc, Floor, Ceiling string }`, and the same for Setting. Then requireDescribed(family string, k KeyDecl) is the only shape. |
| `internal/config/registry.go:41` | high | Registry.claims already maps every claimed key to its family, but membership is answered again by seven linear scans: ownedNow (keys.go:95), reservedClassOf (keys.go:144), integrationClassOf (keys.go:212), supersessionOf (keys.go:189), identitySourceKey (registry.go:405), hasThreshold (thresholds.go:183), and hasSetting (settings.go:207). Each Add* also re-implements the within-family duplicate check. AddReservedClass (registry.go:168-179) restates reservedClassOf's loop, AddIntegrationClass (registry.go:222-228) restates integrationClassOf's predicate verbatim, and AddThreshold (registry.go:250) restates hasThreshold. The `keys[:i]` self-duplicate loop appears three times (registry.go:168, 217, 382). The comment at registry.go:41-46 defends the split as 'two independent checks'. That is a reason to have two checks, not a reason for this code shape. | Make claims the implementation. claim panics on any repeat. AddIdentitySource is the one family that legitimately shares keys, so it skips a key that claims already maps to familyIdentitySource. Add `func (r *Registry) familyOf(key string) (string, bool) { f, ok := r.claims[key]; return f, ok }`. rejectNamespace can then switch on the family, and only the two lookups that must return a payload (the reserved class and the supersession message) keep their own index. |
| `internal/config/registry.go:158` | high | The registry exposes seven per-family getters but does not offer the walk its callers actually want: every environment key with its description and mark. Three callers rebuild that walk by hand, each with its own idea of which families count. cmd/dubplate-server/doctor.go:62-88 and :161-170 do it, and internal/ops/admin/settings.go:82-97 concatenates Flag and Required, then Required and Optional. Adding a family means updating every one of them by hand. | Have the registry own the enumeration: `func (r *Registry) EnvKeys() iter.Seq[KeyDecl]` (stdlib maps.Keys/slices.Values style), yielding wall rows, source keys, class flag and keys, and thresholds in declaration order. Once finding 1 is fixed, every family yields a KeyDecl. |
| `internal/config/thresholds.go:102` | high | There are two bounds implementations with different semantics. Setting's boundedValidator (settings.go:173) parses Floor and Ceiling once, at registration, and requires both. Threshold's checkBounds re-parses them on every ParseInt/ParseDuration/ParseFloat call, treats an empty side as open, and panics at read time on a bad bound. AddThreshold only catches a bad bound as a side effect: validateThresholdDefault (registry.go:280-291) runs a Kind switch that restates the dispatch already in ThresholdKind.Parse (thresholds.go:48) and buildValidator (settings.go:135), and it calls ParseInt(nil, t) to trigger the panic. A KindString threshold with a Floor passes silently. | Build a threshold's validator at AddThreshold the same way AddSetting does. Generalize boundedValidator[T] to allow an open side, store the closure on the Threshold, and have parseThreshold call it. Then checkBounds and the switch in validateThresholdDefault go away, and the Kind-to-type dispatch exists once, in buildValidator. |
| `internal/config/config.go:235` | medium | Lookup (config.go:105) and newWall plus get (config.go:219-241) are two implementations of one rule: last entry wins, and empty means unset. The comment at 231-234 admits it. Single-key readers build a whole wall map to read one key: IntegrationClass.Enabled (keys.go:224), IdentitySource.Selected (registry.go:344), parseThreshold (thresholds.go:134), and ParseString (thresholds.go:200). | Single-key readers should call Lookup. The wall keeps its map for rejectNamespace's walk. wall.integrationEnabled becomes a method on IntegrationClass over Lookup, and the wall calls that method. |
| `internal/config/config.go:459` | medium | (P5) Load writes the four owned key names as literals (config.go:459-462), parallel to the ownedWall table that declares the same four (keys.go:69-91). It then requires them a second time in the reg.ownedWall loop (config.go:472-474), and the wall.missing dedupe map exists to hide that double read. Because the package exports no key constants, other packages declare the literals again: adminEmailKey at cmd/dubplate-server/doctor.go:183 and internal/auth/doctor.go:21, and "DUBPLATE_STATE_DIR" at internal/store/doctor.go:27 and cmd/dubplate-server/unidecode.go:65. | Export the key names as constants (`const StateDirKey = "DUBPLATE_STATE_DIR"`, the pattern intake.StagingKey and backup.RemoteKey already follow), use them in both the table and Load, and replace the downstream literals with them. |
| `internal/config/thresholds.go:14` | medium | (P8) ThresholdKind is also the type of Setting.Kind (settings.go:63), so the name is wrong for half its uses. Its zero value KindInt is valid, justified by history ('a Threshold declared before this field existed', thresholds.go:12-13). Sensitivity (keys.go:21) and ApplyMode (settings.go:22) in the same package use iota+1 so that zero is invalid. validateThresholdDefault (registry.go:261-264) needs a paragraph to explain the workaround, and thresholds_test.go:151 pins the inconsistency in a test. | Name it `Kind`, as slog does with slog.Kind and slog.KindInt64. Start at iota+1 like the package's other two enums, reject the zero value at registration, and delete the history paragraph and TestKindInt_IsZeroValue. |
| `internal/config/thresholds.go:133` | medium | parseThreshold takes a kind argument alongside t.Kind, so there are two sources of truth. ParseInt hard-codes KindInt (thresholds.go:164) whatever the threshold declares. Called on a KindDuration threshold, it reads the value as an int and reports the result as an 'invalid default' registration bug. The exported names add to the confusion. ParseInt, ParseDuration, and ParseFloat borrow strconv's names but resolve a key from the environment. ParseString never parses anything. | Read t.Kind and reject a mismatch with the typed accessor. The flag-style shape reads better as methods: `func (t Threshold) Int(environ []string) (int, error)`, `Duration`, `Float`, `String`. |
| `internal/config/registry.go:159` | low | The vocabulary for one concept is inconsistent. The accessor is OwnedWall() but the adder is AddWallRow, while every other family pairs AddX with Xs. Predicates use four forms: ownedNow, identitySourceKey, hasThreshold, hasSetting. Threshold.Doc and Setting.Doc hold the accepted form, next to a field called Description. ParseAdminAddress sits beside AdminEmail and requireEmail. config.Config stutters; that is common, but it is still the stutter Go Code Review Comments names. | Use WallRows() and has* predicates throughout (hasWallRow, hasIdentitySourceKey). Rename Doc to Form or Usage (flag's term). Use one noun for the admin key (AdminAddress or AdminEmail). |
| `internal/config/thresholds.go:98` | low | checkBounds returns an error that is really a sentence fragment ('the phrase, completing "is ..."'), which callers splice in with %v (thresholds.go:144, 155). The error type is carrying a string. | Return `(phrase string, ok bool)`, or let the finding-3 validator return the final *KeyError reason directly. |

### internal/engine/matcher

Proud: no. Verdict: The package ports beets' scoring core: the Distance accumulator, the album and track components, an exact lapjv reproduction, the candidate set, and the recommendation. The mechanism is careful and deterministic, built from small straight-line components, but much of the exported surface is shaped by the conformance harness rather than by any product caller: `any`-typed options, `*string` for Python's None, string enums re-parsed at every use, a stringly consensus map, and a JSON decoder living in the engine. Comments run at 2.7 times regexp's density, carried by corpus counts, harness line numbers, and rebuttals.

22 findings: 4 high, 9 medium, 9 low. Held by patterns: P1 (4), P2 (7), P3 (2), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/engine/matcher/album.go:110` | medium | compileMediaPatterns and compileCountryPatterns (album.go:204) differ only in the wrapper string and the error text. Both callers then rewrap identically: distanceForAlbum at album.go:40-47 and Validate at tunables.go:462-467 each write `fmt.Errorf("preferred.media: %w: %w", ErrInvalidTunable, err)`. The resulting chain restates its context three times ('preferred.media: invalid tunable: matcher: preferred.media pattern X does not compile: compile option pattern ...'). Validate compiles the patterns and throws them away, and every Candidates.Add compiles them again. | Use one helper that owns the wrap: `func compilePreferred(field, format string, patterns []string) ([]PatternOption, error)`, which returns `fmt.Errorf("%s: %w: %w", field, errInvalidTunable, err)`. Callers propagate it unchanged. Better still, compile once at Validate into the typed config the earlier finding proposes. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/tunables.go:161` | medium | DataSourceMismatchPenalty is read at scoring time by nothing. Its one effect is inside DefaultTunables, which copies it into `DataSourcePenalties["MusicBrainz"]` (tunables.go:320). A caller who sets the field after DefaultTunables changes nothing, and the field's 14-line doc plus the constant's 11-line doc (penalty.go:385-395) explain the relation in prose rather than in code. | Drop the field and let DataSourcePenalties be the single source. If the plugin's resolved penalty must be configurable, it is the map entry. Keep defaultDataSourceMismatchPenalty as the fallback constant with a one-line doc. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/album.go:219` | low | addAlbumLabel, addAlbumCatalogNum, and addAlbumDisambig (album.go:219-244) are one body three times: skip if either side is empty, else AddString. The second branch of addAlbumCountry (album.go:196) is a fourth copy. Each copy carries a 3-line doc that restates the guard. | `func addIfBothSet(dist *Distance, key, likely, cand string)`, called in place at distanceForAlbum's call list, which keeps beets' order visible in one function. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/likelies.go:49` | low | The `str` and `num` closures in MostCommonTags (likelies.go:49-58) have identical bodies and differ only in T, because a Go closure cannot be generic. | Hoist one generic helper: `func likely[T comparable](items []ItemFields, consensus map[string]bool, key string, get func(ItemFields) T) T`. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/penalty.go:67` | low | Methods repeat the receiver type: d.RawDistance(), d.MaxDistance() (penalty.go:82), and d.Distance() (penalty.go:95) on type Distance. GenericPenaltyKeys (penalty.go:183) returns display names, not keys. The string-distance family mixes the `SD` abbreviation (SDPattern, sdStrip, applySDReplace) with the spelled form (StringDist, stringDistBasic, Tunables.StringDistPatterns). addAlbumAlbum (album.go:82) stutters. | Use d.Raw(), d.Max(), and d.Normalized() (or keep Distance and drop the prefix on the other two), and GenericPenaltyNames. Pick one spelling across the family, StringDistPattern or sdPattern. Rename addAlbumAlbum to addAlbumTitle. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/track.go:23` | low | DistanceForTrack's doc spends its first paragraph (track.go:11-13) explaining that the natural name TrackDistance is taken by the pair struct (penalty.go:45). The result pairs exported DistanceForTrack with unexported distanceForAlbum. A comment explaining a naming collision is a sign the collision should be removed. | Rename the pair, for example `type TrackMatch struct{ Info *TrackInfo; Dist *Distance }`, and name the function `TrackDistance`. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/verdict.go:23` | low | Recommendation.String and parseRecommendation (verdict.go:41) are two switches restating the same four-name table in opposite directions. | Use one table, `var recNames = [...]string{RecNone: "none", RecLow: "low", RecMedium: "medium", RecStrong: "strong"}`. String indexes it with a bounds check, and parse loops over it. |

### internal/engine/matcher/unidecode

Proud: no. Verdict: Sound with nits. The table core is close to exemplary: a strict lexer, pinned checksums at both ends, and a deterministic binary format whose layout is documented right where it is encoded. install.go falls below the bar. It carries a test-only Geteuid function field in an exported options struct (the store package already uses the right form), prints to stdout and names CLI flags from inside an engine package, and has inverted error flow and three copies of the read-and-parse step. Those are the patterns an agent would copy next.

12 findings: 1 high, 6 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (2), P3 (2), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/engine/matcher/unidecode/install.go:89` | medium | Three places read the table file and run Parse on it. checkInstalled does it at doctor.go:15-24, Install's already-installed probe repeats it at install.go:89-96, and the read-back does it again at install.go:116-122. BACKLOG #182's size cap and symlink fix therefore has to land in three places. Install also tests leafExists three separate times (lines 79, 89, 103). | Make checkInstalled the single implementation, ideally applying the #182 limited read. The probe becomes `if leafExists && checkInstalled(stateDir) == nil { ... }` and the read-back becomes `if err := checkInstalled(stateDir); err != nil { return fmt.Errorf("verify written table: %w", err) }`. When #159 adds the production loader, this is the natural spot for `Load(stateDir string) (*Table, error)`, which checkInstalled then wraps. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/unidecode/install.go:133` | medium | obtainTable inverts Go's error flow. It nests the success path (`if err == nil { var table []byte; if table, err = Build(wheel); err == nil { return table, nil } }`) and lets two different failures fall through to one shared return. Build is also called in both branches. An agent reading this learns to nest the happy path. | Get the wheel first and build once: `var wheel []byte; var err error; if from != "" { wheel, err = readWheelFile(from); if err != nil { return nil, fmt.Errorf("%s: %w", from, err) } } else { wheel, err = Fetch(ctx, client); if err != nil { return nil, ... } }; return Build(wheel)`. Return early on each error and keep the happy path at the left margin. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/unidecode/pins.go:12` | medium | (P5) The release "1.4.0" is written by hand in four literals that have to stay in sync: Version (pins.go:12), WheelURL's tail (pins.go:16), TableFileName (pins.go:30), and the cache file name in WheelCachePath (fetch.go:60, "Unidecode-1.4.0-py3-none-any.whl", which repeats the last path element of WheelURL). | Derive the ones that can be derived: `const tableFileName = "unidecode-" + Version + ".tbl"`, and `filepath.Join(dir, "unidecode", path.Base(WheelURL))` for the cache. Leave only Version, WheelURL, and the checksums as true pins. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/unidecode/extract.go:69` | low | The non-module branch (lines 69-79) and the module branch (lines 81-88) repeat the same read-to-EOF, close, and two-error sequence and differ only in where the bytes go. dataModuleName also builds the same refusal string twice (lines 125 and 129), checks len(hex) != 3 at line 128 just before isLowerHex3 checks it again, and keeps a ParseUint error path (lines 132-134) that isLowerHex3 has already made unreachable. | Extract `func readEntry(f *zip.File) ([]byte, error)` that opens, reads all, and closes. The wheel is capped at 1 MiB, so reading non-modules into memory costs nothing, and the loop becomes `content, err := readEntry(f); ...; if !isModule { continue }`. In dataModuleName, compute the refusal once, drop the redundant length test, and parse the three hex digits without the unreachable branch. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/unidecode/lex.go:105` | low | next() switches on '=', '(', ')', ',' at line 132 and then calls punctuationKind, which switches on the same four bytes again and quietly returns tokComma for any other byte. Errors are also built two ways: l.errf uses the current position, while six sites (lines 172, 186, 242, 268, 281, 292) spell out fmt.Errorf("%s:%d:%d: ...", file, line, col) by hand. | Use one table, `var punct = map[byte]tokenKind{'=': tokEquals, '(': tokLParen, ')': tokRParen, ',': tokComma}`, and in next write `if k, ok := punct[b]; ok { ... }`. Add `func (l *lexer) errAt(line, col int, format string, args ...any) error` and let errf call it with l.line and l.col. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/unidecode/pins.go:43` | low | unidecodeMaxCodepoint stutters against the package name. lexModule (lex.go:234) parses rather than lexes, and its own doc comment says "parses". dataModuleName (extract.go:117) is a noun naming a function that parses and validates. Fetch and ReadWheel are a pair, but only one of them names its object. In table.go:181 and 185, decode's locals sweepLo and sweepHi shadow the package constants of the same name, which are also the Table field names. Test names mix a stuttering underscore style (TestUnidecodeInstall_RefusesAbsentStateDir, install_test.go:55) with plain CamelCase (TestBuildIsByteIdenticalToThePin). | Rename to maxCodepoint, parseModule, parseModuleName, and FetchWheel (or keep Fetch and rename ReadWheel to Read). Rename the decode locals to lo and hi. Name the tests TestInstallRefusesAbsentStateDir and so on. |

### internal/engine/matcher/unidecode/unidecodetest

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. This is a small, well-named xxxtest helper that loads the pinned Unidecode table through the same checksum-checked Build and Parse path production uses. Its one exported function takes a testing.TB, but none of its three callers has one: all three are TestMain functions, and each had to copy an identical mainTB adapter that wraps a nil testing.TB.

5 findings: 1 high, 1 medium, 3 low. Held by patterns: P1 (2), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/engine/matcher/unidecode/unidecodetest/unidecodetest.go:15` | low | In LoadCachedTable, "Cached" describes the table, but the cache holds the wheel. Every call reads the wheel and rebuilds the table, so a reader of `unidecodetest.LoadCachedTable(...)` would expect a stored table to be read back. The name does not stutter against the package name, but it misdescribes the work. | Choose a name that says what is cached, for example `unidecodetest.TableFromCachedWheel()`. Or, since the package name already supplies the context, use `unidecodetest.Table()`, the way httptest.NewServer and iotest.ErrReader lean on their package names. |
| `internal/engine/matcher/unidecode/unidecodetest/unidecodetest.go:22` | low | (P7) Messages written by package unidecodetest carry the prefix "unidecode:" (:22, :26, :30), and the test pins that prefix (unidecodetest_test.go:44). By Go convention an error's prefix names the package that produced it. Here the messages come from unidecodetest, so a reader looking for where a message comes from is sent to the parent package. Separately, :19 uses `tb.Fatalf("%v", err)` where `tb.Fatal(err)` says the same thing. | Prefix with "unidecodetest:", or drop the prefix and let the wrapped unidecode errors carry theirs. Use tb.Fatal(err), or return err once the signature returns an error. |

### internal/engine/musicbrainz

Proud: no. Verdict: Sound with nits. The mechanism is good stdlib Go: a package-level sleep seam, a byte cap that fails instead of truncating, one validate boundary, and an ordered criteria walk. Exported surface costs it the most: thirteen wire types, AlbumSearch's two fields, and ErrMalformedRelease have no reader outside the package. Comments run at twice the density of net/smtp, much of it test process and rebuttals, and the open #167 already logged most of this without any change since.

12 findings: 1 high, 6 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P2 (3), P3 (1), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/engine/musicbrainz/client.go:248` | medium | (P7) The sentinels do not match their own uses. SearchReleases wraps a search-body decode failure in ErrMalformedRelease, though a search body is not a release. doWithRetry returns ErrNotFound, whose text is 'release not found', on a search 404 as well. The messages also mix three styles: 'release not found', 'musicbrainz unavailable' and 'malformed musicbrainz release' for the sentinels, the 'musicbrainz: ' prefix on the wrapped errors, and no prefix at all on validate's errors (release.go:186, 191, 197, 207). The stdlib convention is one 'pkg: ' prefix, as in `http.ErrServerClosed = errors.New("http: Server closed")`. | Name each sentinel for what it actually covers, for example errMalformed or ErrMalformedResponse, and give every sentinel and returned error one prefix: `errors.New("musicbrainz: not found")`, `errors.New("musicbrainz: unavailable")`, and `fmt.Errorf("musicbrainz: relation %d has empty target-type: %w", ...)`. |
| `internal/engine/musicbrainz/tracks.go:70` | medium | buildTrack searches recording.Aliases twice: once at :70 to decide whether tr.Title may override, and again through keyWithPreferredAlias at :73. Its doc (tracks.go:51-56) explains the double read instead of removing it. credit.go:42-46 does the opposite and inlines keyWithPreferredAlias by hand. That leaves one rule, 'an alias name or else a fallback', with three spellings in the package. | In tracks.go: `title := cmp.Or(tr.Title, recording.Title); title = keyWithPreferredAlias(recording.Aliases, title, tun)`, which is one search with the same result. In credit.go: `artist.WriteString(keyWithPreferredAlias(part.Artist.Aliases, part.Artist.Name, tun))`. |
| `internal/engine/musicbrainz/client.go:277` | low | AlbumForID writes the fetch-then-translate pair twice (:277-284 and :294-301), and the two copies differ only in how they wrap the error. | Extract `func (c *Client) albumInfo(ctx context.Context, id string, tun matcher.Tunables) (*Release, *matcher.AlbumInfo, error)` and call it twice. The second call wraps its error with the pseudo-release context. |
| `internal/engine/musicbrainz/credit.go:19` | low | The credits struct and applyVariousArtists (credit.go:58-64) exist to carry the first artist's id to one string comparison. The track path, tracks.go:75, builds the struct and throws ArtistID away. A two-field type plus a (credits, bool) function is a lot of machinery for `parts[0].Artist.ID == variousArtistsID`. | Have parseArtistCredits return a string, and decide VA in TranslateAlbum: `artist := parseArtistCredits(rel.ArtistCredit, tun); va := rel.ArtistCredit[0].Artist.ID == variousArtistsID; if va { artist = tun.VAName }`. Then delete credits and applyVariousArtists. |
| `internal/engine/musicbrainz/query.go:64` | low | AlbumSearchFor uses a 'For' suffix, a Python-flavored constructor name that Go APIs avoid. SearchReleases (client.go:236) returns []string ids, not releases. Parameter names drift across files: Medium is `m` in tracks.go:37 and 57 but `medium` in tracks.go:163 and release.go:194, and Track is `tr` in tracks.go:57 but `track` in tracks.go:179 and release.go:195. | Rename to NewAlbumSearch(items, tun) and SearchReleaseIDs. Pick one short name per type across the package, either m and tr or medium and track. |
| `internal/engine/musicbrainz/release.go:205` | low | validate walks rel.Relations twice, at :184-188 for an empty target-type and again at :205-209 for a missing transl target. retryWait's `retryAfter == ""` branch at client.go:379-381 repeats work the next branch already does: strconv.Atoi("") fails and falls through to backoff. | Check both relation conditions in one loop. Delete the empty-string branch in retryWait. |

### internal/engine/tag

Proud: no. Verdict: This is a small, well-placed package: it is a faithful port of beets' tag_album that takes the concrete MusicBrainz client and an evalDate parameter, with no test seam or clock read, and it separates 404 drops from real failures with care. What costs it most is the exported surface: ErrNoCandidates turns an empty result into an error, so the only caller has to rebuild the verdict Album should have returned, and Proposal exports two fields nothing reads. The result-building tail is also written twice, and the comment density (0.62 against cookiejar's 0.35) comes from rebuttals and repeated sentences.

8 findings: 1 high, 4 medium, 3 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (2), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/engine/tag/doc.go:8` | medium | The package name `tag` sits next to `tagio`, which does handle tags. This package matches albums against MusicBrainz and touches no tags. doc.go:8-10 spends a paragraph explaining that the pairing is deliberate, and tagio/doc.go:6 has to point back at it. A name that needs a defending paragraph in two packages is the wrong name. The call reads as `tag.Album`, where a Go reader expects tag data. | Name the package for what it does. `autotag`, beets' own module name, keeps the lineage and reads well at the call site (`autotag.Album(ctx, client, items, ...)`). Then delete both disambiguation paragraphs. |
| `internal/engine/tag/tag.go:73` | medium | Two blocks repeat the same steps: Recommend, wrap the error, ActionFor, wrap the error, then a five-field Proposal literal. One is the early-return block (tag.go:73-89) and the other is the tail (tag.go:111-125). The only difference is which candidate slice they use. That pair accounts for four of the six identical `fmt.Errorf("tag: %w", err)` wraps. | Write the steps once in an unexported helper and call it from both places: `func propose(tun matcher.Tunables, cands []matcher.Candidate) (*Proposal, error) { rec, err := matcher.Recommend(tun, cands); ...; action, err := matcher.ActionFor(tun, rec); ...; return &Proposal{Candidates: cands, Recommendation: rec, Action: action}, nil }`. ActionFor is pure, so computing it before the early-return test costs nothing: `p, err := propose(tun, cands.All()); if err != nil {...}; if !tun.Timid && p.Recommendation == matcher.RecStrong { return p, nil }`. This also removes the redundant `cands.Len() > 0` at tag.go:77, because RecStrong already implies a candidate. |
| `internal/engine/tag/tag.go:133` | low | lookupAndAdd takes seven parameters, and both call sites (tag.go:63, :102) pass the same six with only the id changing. The client is named `src` although it is a concrete *musicbrainz.Client with no source abstraction behind it. The only caller names the same value `client`. | Inside Album, bind the six constant values once: `add := func(mbid string) error { ... }`, or keep the helper and group ctx, client, cands, tun, items, and evalDate in a small unexported struct. Rename `src` to `client` in both signatures. |

### internal/engine/tagio

Proud: no. Verdict: Sound with nits. tagio is a FLAC metadata reader and writer built around one strong idea: a declarative 69-row field table driven by a small generic codec (field.go:35, :141). Around it sits a bounded block walk that refuses a block from its declared length before allocating anything, and an atomic streaming rewrite. Exported surface and naming cost it the most. Serialize and KeyWrite are exported for a test in another package. The exported TagSet spells MBTrackid, Isrc and Bpm right beside AcoustIDID, which is the pattern the next added field will copy. Comment density is 0.48 against archive/tar at 0.30.

15 findings: 2 high, 6 medium, 7 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (5), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/engine/tagio/tagset.go:77` | high | TagSet's Go names copy the ledger's spelling instead of Go's initialism rule (Go Code Review Comments, Initialisms), and they disagree with each other within one struct. AcoustIDID (:34) and R128AlbumGain get the casing right. MBAlbumartistid, MBAlbumid, MBArtistid, MBReleasegroupid, MBTrackid, MBWorkid and MBAlbumartistids (:77-85) do not, and neither do Isrc (:72), Asin (:53), Bpm (:55) or RgAlbumGain/RgTrackPeak (:93-96). Compound words are also run together (Albumartist, Albumdisambig, Tracktotal, Disctotal, Catalognum) where mixedCaps wants AlbumArtist and TrackTotal. The JSON tag already carries the ledger name, so the Go name is free to be idiomatic. It is an exported type that the gate and the fixture spell, and the next field added will copy whichever spelling sits next to it. | Rename to MBAlbumArtistID, MBAlbumID, MBArtistID, MBReleaseGroupID, MBTrackID, MBWorkID, MBAlbumArtistIDs, ISRC, ASIN, BPM, RGAlbumGain, AlbumArtist, AlbumArtistCredit, TrackTotal, DiscTotal, CatalogNum and so on, and keep the json tags as they are. Update the field.go table accessors and fieldStructName in tagset_test.go in the same change. |
| `internal/engine/tagio/container.go:147` | medium | The block-count bound (more than 1024 blocks) returns ErrTagsTooLarge, a sentinel whose message is 'tag block too large'. The chain-total bound returns ErrPictureTooLarge (:152). Bounds.PictureBytes (:119) quietly means two things, the picture cap and a quarter of the chain cap. A gate that rejects on ErrTagsTooLarge tells a member their tags are too big when the real problem is a chain of tiny empty blocks. A reader of Bounds cannot see the chain cap without reading admit. | Give the chain its own sentinel and field, for example `ErrChainTooLarge` and `Bounds.ChainBytes`, or at minimum rename PictureBytes' second role into the type. Each sentinel's name should match every case that returns it. |
| `internal/engine/tagio/container.go:193` | medium | parse (:173) and readContainer (:193) repeat the same open, signature read, stat and parseBlocks sequence. They differ in the bounds argument and in how a non-FLAC file is reported. parse also turns an I/O error from readSignature into ErrUnsupportedContainer (:185-186), so the two entry points give different error contracts for one condition. Write (write.go:46) then calls Sniff and Parse, opening the file twice to get exactly the error readContainer was written to give, and copyFrameRegion (write.go:241) opens it a third time with a bare os.Open that repeats openFile (sniff.go:45). | Keep one `parse(path string, bounds *Bounds)` that returns readSignature's error unchanged. Parse, ParseBounded and Read all go through it, and readContainer goes away. Write drops its Sniff call and calls parse directly, and copyFrameRegion uses openFile. |
| `internal/engine/tagio/picture.go:111` | medium | pictureField is vorbisField (vorbis.go:74) with a different byte order and a label. Its own doc comment calls it 'the big-endian counterpart of vorbisField'. The encode side already merges the pair into one function that takes a byte order (appendLengthPrefixed, write.go:230). The decode side does not, so a fix to the overflow check has to land in two places. | Add `func cutLengthPrefixed(order binary.ByteOrder, b []byte) (field, rest []byte, err error)` beside appendLengthPrefixed. decodeVorbisComment and decodePicture call it with binary.LittleEndian and binary.BigEndian and wrap the error with their own label, as they already do with the index. |
| `internal/engine/tagio/sniff.go:17` | medium | (P8) FormatFLAC is iota, so Format's zero value is FLAC. Sniff's failure returns (`return 0, err` at :36 and :40) therefore read as 'FLAC, plus an error', and a zero Format in gate.policy (policy.go:34) is indistinguishable from an explicit FLAC row. A second format arriving under the existing 'a second format brings its own constant' plan inherits the ambiguity. | Reserve the zero value: `const ( formatUnknown Format = iota; FormatFLAC )`, or start at `iota + 1`, so the zero value is never a supported format. |
| `internal/engine/tagio/encode.go:141` | low | valueOr takes no fallback argument, yet its name promises one, and it has no doc comment. style (field.go:9) names what is really one Vorbis key with a writable flag, borrowing mediafile's StorageStyle vocabulary where a Go reader expects key. | Rename valueOr to deref or orZero, or use a generic `func deref[T any](p *T) T` that the gate's own deref could share. Consider renaming style to key or vorbisKey. |
| `internal/engine/tagio/write.go:76` | low | writeBlock is a type named like a function, and it sits beside the function writeBlocks (:189) and emitBlock (:209). It also carries exported field names on an unexported type, and it restates Block's Type and Payload. Reading `writeBlocks(w, blocks []writeBlock)` takes a second pass. | Once Length is gone, reuse Block (Last is ignored on emit, since writeBlocks sets the flag itself), or rename the type to a noun such as pendingBlock with unexported fields. |

### internal/harness

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. This is a test-only gate package that checks the committed corpus and its ledgers through the cassette and analysis loaders, and the checks themselves are careful: one subtest per case, a round-trip through the production writer, ledgers checked in both directions. What costs it most is the boundary. Because the file sits in a parent package of its own, it is the only reason nine analysis functions are exported, and it enforces cassette's case invariants in a test where the sibling subsonic/session loader enforces its own.

8 findings: 2 high, 4 medium, 2 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/corpus_test.go:106` | high | Three case-format invariants live only in this gate test, not in the loader that owns the format: no duplicate (method, path_query) key (106-120), no orphan response file (126-148), and the schema version pin (156, 167). cassette.LoadCase (cassette.go:121) and LoadManifest (cassette.go:248) accept all three violations, and stub.New silently overwrites a duplicate key (stub.go:70). The sibling capture format does it the other way: subsonic/session.Load owns its schemaVersion const (session.go:45, checked at 150), orphan detection (186), and duplicate detection (322-323). The module now has two patterns for the same job, and this file holds the weaker one. | Put the rules in cassette next to the type: `const schemaVersion = "4"` checked in LoadManifest, plus a `seen := map[key]bool{}` duplicate check and a responses/ directory sweep in Case.validate, written the way session.Load is. Then delete the three tests here. cassette_test.go already tests the loader's rejections. |
| `internal/harness/corpus_test.go:242` | medium | (P9) Three tests collect per-case results into a map from inside forEachCase's subtests and then check a ledger against that map (got at 242-251, cases at 310-313 and 336-339). When one case's LoadCase fails, its subtest calls Fatalf, the map is missing that id, and the ledger check then reports a second, misleading error such as a stale acknowledgment or a row naming no committed case. One defect then fails under two tests with the wrong diagnosis. | Load the case set once, without subtests, in a helper that fails the test outright, such as `func corpusCases(t *testing.T) map[string]*cassette.Case`. Use forEachCase only for checks that really are per case. |
| `internal/harness/corpus_test.go:427` | medium | (P9) TestLoadCaseRejectsCorruptedFixture repeats cassette_test.go:258 TestLoadCaseCorruptedFixture: same fixture, same nil check, same "eval_date" substring. It also reaches into another package's testdata (line 428). Its rationale, guarding against a pass over an empty corpus, is already met by corpusCaseDirs at lines 58-60. TestManifestParsesAndMatchesSchemaVersion (161) overlaps cassette_test.go:268 TestLoadManifest in the same way, since both load the committed manifest. | Delete TestLoadCaseRejectsCorruptedFixture. Once the schema pin moves into LoadManifest, cassette_test.go's TestLoadManifest covers line 161 as well. |
| `internal/harness/corpus_test.go:7` | low | The package clause and doc comment sit in a _test.go file, where go doc never shows them. Inside the package, one concept goes by three names (CorpusCase at 82 and 262, CommittedCase at 172, Case), and the ledger tests mix Ledger, Table, and Blocks. TestAdjudicationsLedgerIsWellFormed wraps analysis.LoadAdjudications, which has no Ledger in its name, and local variables are named ledger, adjLedger, and bugs for parallel things. forEachCase passes id, dir, and c, but after line 89 id equals filepath.Base(dir) equals c.ID, so most callers discard a parameter with _. | Use the moddeps form, `package harness_test` with no package doc, or no file here once the move above lands. Settle on one noun (corpus case) and one suffix (Ledger) across test names, and narrow the callback to `func(t *testing.T, dir string, c *cassette.Case)`. |
| `internal/harness/corpus_test.go:269` | low | The same four-line loop that turns case directories into ids appears at 269-272, 359-362, and 379-382. The adjudications and oracle-bugs load pair appears at 384-391 and 409-416. The expression `filepath.Join(root, "corpus", "analysis", name)` is written nine times, and the c.Expected nil guard three times (92, 222, 243). TestComponentTableIsWellFormed (289) repeats the CheckComponentTable call conform/component_test.go:77 already makes on the same committed file. | Add `func corpusCaseIDs(t *testing.T) []string` and an `analysisFile(name string) string` helper, load the two ledgers once in a shared helper, and keep one CheckComponentTable call site (conform's, which needs the table anyway). |

### internal/harness/analysis

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The package reads strict JSON into one struct per committed ledger and reports every failure at once. That mechanism is sound, and the self-consistency checks (recorded gap against recorded costs, thresholds read from the record) show real judgment. It falls below the bar at its boundaries: the Load functions push every ledger's file name onto about 30 call sites in three packages, the status values are untyped strings named inconsistently, the disposition rule is written out twice, several exported constants have no outside reader, and the comments carry process history the code cannot use.

12 findings: 3 high, 5 medium, 4 low. Held by patterns: P2 (3), P3 (1), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/analysis/adjudication.go:17` | high | (P8) Four ledgers carry closed sets of string values (dispositions, tie dispositions, proximity statuses, reproduced values). All are untyped string constants on plain string fields, and only some are prefixed. Ties, proximity and reproduced use TieExcluded, ProximityFlagged and ReproducedYes, but the dispositions are bare: Pending, OracleWrong, OracleCorrect, NoCorrectAnswer. At the call site, conform/case.go:950 reads `case analysis.Pending:`, which says nothing about what is pending. Because the fields are plain strings, any string compiles where a disposition belongs, and the valid set has to be restated as a switch at every validation site (adjudication.go:212, oraclebug.go:142). New ledgers will copy this pattern. | Give each set a named string type, the way stdlib names closed sets: `type Disposition string; const ( DispositionPending Disposition = "pending-adjudication"; DispositionOracleWrong ...)`, with `Disposition Disposition `json:"disposition"`` on both entry types and one `func (d Disposition) valid() bool`. Do the same for TieDisposition, ProximityStatus and Reproduced. encoding/json decodes named string types with no extra code. |
| `internal/harness/analysis/album_info.go:44` | medium | (P5) AlbumInfoPseudoSubjects is a second list of case ids that must be kept in agreement with AlbumInfoSubjects by hand. CheckAlbumInfoLedger only walks AlbumInfoSubjects and asks slices.Contains(AlbumInfoPseudoSubjects, ...) (album_info.go:227-236). So a pseudo id that is misspelled, or is missing from AlbumInfoSubjects, is never checked, and the parallel list can drift without any error. | Fold the expectation into the table it describes: `type AlbumInfoSubject struct { CaseID, Why string; Pseudo bool }`, and check `s.Pseudo` in the merged loop. It is still a named expectation, written by hand rather than derived from the rows, so the comment's reason survives. corpus_test.go's one reference moves to the field. |
| `internal/harness/analysis/oraclebug.go:42` | medium | The function names do not follow one rule. Five ledgers use Load<Type> and Check<Type> (LoadTieLedger, CheckTieLedger, LoadComponentTable). Two use a plural noun instead: LoadAdjudications/CheckAdjudications for AdjudicationLedger, and LoadOracleBugs/CheckOracleBugs for OracleBugLedger. Names also collide across files. AlbumInfoLedger (the file), AlbumInfoLedgerRow (a row) and LedgerAlbumInfo (album_info.go:104, the mirrored matcher.AlbumInfo) are permutations of the same three words, and LedgerTrackInfo follows the third. Row types carry 'Ledger' (AlbumInfoLedgerRow, FileTagLedgerRow) while entry types do not (TieEntry, ProximityEntry). ComponentTable.Beets (component.go:47) holds what every other header calls BeetsVersion. | Use one rule: LoadAdjudicationLedger/CheckAdjudicationLedger and LoadOracleBugLedger/CheckOracleBugLedger. Rename the mirror types after what they mirror: AlbumInfoRecord and TrackInfoRecord, or BeetsAlbumInfo and BeetsTrackInfo. Rename rows to AlbumInfoRow and FileTagRow. Use `BeetsVersion string `json:"beets"``, since the Go name need not copy the JSON key. |
| `internal/harness/analysis/oraclebug.go:141` | medium | checkOracleBugEntry's last block (oraclebug.go:141-148) repeats adjudication.go:211-219 statement for statement: the same four-way disposition switch with an early return, and the same 'only a human name may set anything but Pending' rule against AgentAdjudicator. Only the error prefix differs. The 'id missing / listed twice' loop with its `known` set is also duplicated between CheckAdjudications (adjudication.go:165-182) and CheckOracleBugs (oraclebug.go:65-82). The owner-only rule is the one rule the two ledgers must agree on, and today two copies hold it. | Keep one implementation in adjudication.go and have both checkers call it: `func checkRuling(ledger, id string, d Disposition, adjudicator string) []error`. With the typed Disposition above, the switch becomes `if !d.valid()`. A small `func caseSet(ids []string) map[string]bool` replaces the three copies of the set-building loop at adjudication.go:166, oraclebug.go:66 and ties.go:97. |
| `internal/harness/analysis/album_info.go:190` | low | Error messages and comments hard-code the length of a table the package already has: "not one of the nineteen named AlbumInfo subjects" (album_info.go:190), "not one of the twelve named file-tags subjects" (file_tags.go:144), and "which is 50 rows" (album_info.go:52). Adding one subject makes all three wrong. The row floors also restate the subject check. Every subject must already have a row (file_tags.go:162-166, album_info.go:207-224), so FileTagLedgerRowFloor = 12 can only fail when FileTagSubjects itself shrinks. Its comment gives a different reason ("the derivation quietly dropped a subject"). | Format the count from the table, `fmt.Errorf("...: not one of the %d named subjects", len(FileTagSubjects))`, or drop it. If the floors stay, say what they actually guard: "pins the length of FileTagSubjects so a deleted subject fails review." |
| `internal/harness/analysis/proximity.go:64` | low | `analysis.Flag(exp)` is a bare verb whose object is implied. Its result type is Proximity and it may return ProximityClear, so at the call site (corpus_test.go:246) the name says less than the type it returns. Every other entry point in the package names its subject (CheckTieLedger, LoadClaims). Its one error (proximity.go:67) is also the only error in the package with no ledger prefix. | Use `func MeasureProximity(exp *cassette.Expected) (Proximity, error)` (or ProximityOf), and prefix the error "proximity: record echoes no thresholds ..." to match its siblings. |

### internal/harness/cassette

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. This is a small, well-bounded schema-and-I/O package with a careful canonicalization rule, and every exported type and function except LoadManifest has production callers in cmd/harness or internal/harness. What costs it most is naming: (*Case).Write takes a name Effective Go reserves for io.Writer, and it pairs with LoadCase under mismatched verbs. Comment density is about twice the stdlib's, and the package doc and helpers spend that density rebutting designs nobody wrote.

9 findings: 0 high, 4 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P4 (3).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/cassette/cassette.go:121` | medium | LoadCase's validation errors do not name the case they came from: "case.json: id: missing", "case.json: requests[0]: ..." (lines 142-162). Three cmd/harness callers wrap them only with "load case: %w" (capture.go:206, freeze.go:60, seeditems.go:74), so a corrupt case prints no path. The conform callers patch over this by adding the dir themselves (conform/case.go:327 "case at %s"), which shows the gap. The stdlib convention, os.PathError and json's file-reading callers, is that the function that knows the path puts it in the error. The validate messages also hard-code "case.json" six times while caseFileName exists for exactly that string. | Compute `path := filepath.Join(dir, caseFileName)` once and prefix every LoadCase error with it: `return nil, fmt.Errorf("%s: %w", path, err)` around c.validate. validate then returns field-level errors ("id: missing", "requests[%d]: ...") without the literal file name. |
| `internal/harness/cassette/cassette.go:172` | medium | The doc for checkResponseReadable says it "proves name resolves to a readable file under dir", and LoadCase's doc (line 120) says the same. The code is `os.ReadFile(filepath.Join(dir, name))`, so a response name like "../other-case/responses/001.json" passes while resolving outside dir. It also reads every response body in full, then discards it, just to prove the file opens. | Keep the stated contract with the stdlib tools for it: `if !filepath.IsLocal(name) { return fmt.Errorf("response %q: not within case dir", name) }`, then `f, err := os.Open(filepath.Join(dir, name)); if err == nil { f.Close() }`. Alternatively, os.OpenInRoot(dir, name) does both in one call. The rationale comment about discarding the bytes then goes away. |
| `internal/harness/cassette/cassette.go:185` | medium | func (c *Case) Write(dir string) error takes the name Effective Go reserves for io.Writer: "Read, Write, Close, Flush, String and so on have canonical signatures and meanings... don't give your method one of those names unless it has the same signature and meaning." It also pairs with a package-level LoadCase under different verbs and different shapes (a function for loading, a method for saving). The rest of the repo already uses the package-function form (tagio.Write(full, ...) in internal/harness/subsonic/fixture/build.go:46, session.Write(cfg.out, s) in cmd/harness/recordoracle.go:221), so the package is also inconsistent with its neighbours. Five production callers (cmd/harness/capture.go:179,237, freeze.go:78, seeditems.go:98, synth.go:79) are already copying the shape. | Use one verb pair in one shape: `func LoadCase(dir string) (*Case, error)` and `func SaveCase(dir string, c *Case) error`, or rename both to ReadCase/WriteCase. Either way it becomes a package function, so `c.Write(dir)` turns into `cassette.SaveCase(dir, c)`. |
| `internal/harness/cassette/cassette.go:69` | low | Expected is an adjective used as a type name. It reads fine as the field Case.Expected, but as a type in signatures it reads oddly: `func Derive(...) (*cassette.Expected, error)` (oracle/oracle.go:109) and `CheckClaims(exp *cassette.Expected, ...)` (analysis/claims.go:86). Go type names are nouns, and the package's own doc calls it "the oracle record". | Rename the type to a noun such as `Outcome` or `Expectation` and keep the field name `Expected *Outcome`. |
| `internal/harness/cassette/cassette.go:221` | low | ReleaseLookup(mbid) string reads as though it performs a lookup, but it returns a canonical path-and-query string. Its siblings already use the precise noun (ReleaseLookupPrefix, Request.PathQuery, CanonicalizePathQuery). maximalReleaseInclude (213) is singular, while beets' RELEASE_INCLUDES and the client's releaseIncludes (internal/engine/musicbrainz/client.go:40) are plural. | `func ReleaseLookupPathQuery(mbid string) string` and `const maximalReleaseIncludes = ...`. |

### internal/harness/conform

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. conform replays the components table, the translation ledger and every corpus case against the engine, and it counts only the comparisons it actually made, so a skipped case shows up as a shortfall against a floor. That mechanism is careful. What costs it most is the exported surface: no package imports conform, all 25 of its exported identifiers are used only by its own tests, and the per-case grading, the compat-class mapping and the float comparison are each written more than once.

19 findings: 3 high, 9 medium, 7 low. Held by patterns: P1 (3), P2 (2), P3 (1), P4 (4).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/conform/case.go:321` | high | gradeCase and gradeCaseDefault use named results, a deferred `err = errors.Join(errs...)`, and early exits written as `return res, nil` (case.go:330, 335, 346, 352, 365, 372 and the matching lines in gradeCaseDefault). These returns actually return an error, and the visible nil says the opposite. An agent copying the shape will read `return x, nil` as success. | Return the error you have: `return res, fmt.Errorf(...)` for a setup failure. Collect the comparison errors in a local slice and end with `return res, errors.Join(errs...)`. Keep a deferred join only for the Close error: `defer func() { err = errors.Join(err, srv.Close()) }()`. |
| `internal/harness/conform/case.go:519` | high | gradeCaseDefault (case.go:519-569) repeats gradeCase's prologue (case.go:321-366) almost line for line: the named-result defer join, LoadCase, the nil Expected check, policyFor, caseInputs, stub.New with a deferred Close, and gradeClient, all with the same error strings. The two bodies differ only in the pending early return and the Validate call. A third grading mode would copy the block again. | One helper owns the setup and the teardown: `func openCase(dir string, led ledgers) (*caseRun, error)` returns a caseRun holding c, exp, policy, evalDate, tun, items, srv and client, plus `func (r *caseRun) Close() error`. Each mode then starts with `run, err := openCase(dir, led); if err != nil { return res, err }; defer func() { err = errors.Join(err, run.Close()) }()`. |
| `internal/harness/conform/case.go:904` | medium | policyFor takes the four ledgers as four parameters even though the ledgers struct (case.go:226) exists to group them. Both production call sites unpack it field by field (case.go:339, 537). It also rebuilds led.adj.ByCase(), a fresh map over every entry, once per case (case.go:944), and gradeCaseDefault rebuilds it again (case.go:604). | Write it as a method on the bundle: `func (l ledgers) policy(caseID string) casePolicy`. Have loadLedgers build the by-case index once and store it as a field on ledgers. |
| `internal/harness/conform/case.go:986` | medium | (P5) The pairing of the hypothesis id bug-remix-parenthetical with Compat.RemixParenthetical is written in three places. widenCompat switches on the id string (case.go:986-991), CheckDefaultCompatMatchesTheLedger repeats it in a one-row literal table (case.go:697-702), and compatFor ORs each Compat field by hand (case.go:493-495). A second compat class needs three edits that must agree, and nothing fails if one is missed. | One table is the implementation: `var compatClasses = []struct{ hypothesisID string; on func(*matcher.Compat) *bool }{{"bug-remix-parenthetical", func(c *matcher.Compat) *bool { return &c.RemixParenthetical }}}`. widenCompat, the ledger check and the widening all range over it. |
| `internal/harness/conform/component.go:24` | medium | (P5) Three hand-kept lists name the same method set: componentMethods (component.go:24-36), the switch in ReplayRow (:112-137), and the adderCalls keys (:199-207). The switch's default branch is commented 'Unreachable', which shows the slice check above it restates the switch. | Make the dispatch a table: `var replayers = map[string]func(analysis.ComponentRow) ReplayOutcome{"add": replayAddMethod, "string_dist": replayStringDist, ...}`. ReplayRow becomes a lookup that fails on a missing key, and the coverage test ranges over `slices.Sorted(maps.Keys(replayers))`. The cross-check against analysis's own list still holds. |
| `internal/harness/conform/component.go:828` | medium | The package has two near-miss comparators that use different tolerance formulas. compareFloat (case.go:849) scales by max(\|want\|,\|got\|) through nearMissTolerance. compareFloats (component.go:828) scales by max(\|want\|,1) and hardcodes 1e-12 at component.go:833 and again in the message at :818. It also has two near-miss records: the NearMiss struct (case.go:85), and ReplayOutcome.NearMiss bool plus Detail string (component.go:51-57). The names differ by one letter and the behavior differs. | Keep one comparator in a shared file, something like `func withinTolerance(want, got float64) (exact, near bool)` built on nearMissTolerance, and one NearMiss{ID, Field, Want, Got} record. Have both tiers call it and report it. |
| `internal/harness/conform/translate.go:372` | medium | Listing the corpus case directories is written three times: GradeCases (case.go:162-176), checkNoReleaseReachesBrowseThreshold (translate.go:372-384), and the test helper corpusCaseDirs (defaults_test.go:22). Each copy runs ReadDir, filters with IsDir, and joins the path. | Keep one `func caseDirs(root string) ([]string, error)` and have the two sweeps and the test helper call it. |
| `internal/harness/conform/case.go:686` | low | The exported names follow no single pattern. CheckDefaultCompatMatchesTheLedger reads like a test name, article included. The two summaries order their words differently (CaseGradeSummary next to ReplaySummary). The comparators compareFloat and compareFloats differ by one letter. The type NearMiss (case.go:85) shares its name with the bool field ReplayOutcome.NearMiss (component.go:54). In unexported structs, casePolicy's fields are capitalized (case.go:882) while caseGradeResult's are lowercase (case.go:259). checkNullable's error text prints a Go identifier, 'which nullStrict forbids' (translate.go:61). There is no stutter against the package name. | After unexporting, use one verb per tier: checkCompatLedger; caseSummary next to replaySummary; one comparator under one name. Give unexported structs lowercase fields throughout, and make error messages describe the rule rather than name the constant. |
| `internal/harness/conform/component.go:102` | low | ReplayRow recovers any panic and turns it into a row error, which drops the matcher's stack trace at the point a programming bug would most need it.
