Separately, policyFor's proximity-skipped and tie-skipped branches repeat the same three assignments (case.go:922-924, 933-935). | Let a panic propagate, or include debug.Stack() in the wrapped error. Fold the skipped-branch assignments into a small helper, `p.skipDistances()`. |

### internal/harness/oracle

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The package runs beets through a Python shim against a replay stub, and its core Derive path has real judgment: it returns raw stdout for the determinism diff, checks key presence on the raw document, and writes the miss file whatever the shim's outcome. It loses the most on exported surface: 8 of its 11 exports exist only for same-package golden-update tests, and five ledger drivers each copy the same stub, scratch-dir and profile prologue, so the files read as one per task shipped.

16 findings: 3 high, 7 medium, 6 low. Held by patterns: P1 (2), P2 (2), P3 (1), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/oracle/ties.go:56` | high | The derivation prologue is copied three times: resolve dir, stub.New plus deferred Close, MkdirTemp plus deferred RemoveAll, renderProfile, then write config.yaml. The copies sit at oracle.go:154-182 (deriveRaw), album_info.go:41-69 and ties.go:56-83, and they differ only in the scratch prefix and the error prefix. 'Marshal an envelope and write scratch/input.json' appears five times (oracle.go:280, album_info.go:71, ties.go:86, defect_free.go:101, file_tags.go:41). The block sits below dupl's 150-token threshold but it is the same block, and the next ledger will copy it again. | Make deriveRaw's prologue the implementation and have the others call it. One shape is `type session struct{ scratch, profile string; srv *stub.Server }` with `func openSession(c *cassette.Case, dir string) (*session, error)` and `Close()`, plus `func writeJSON(dir, name string, v any) (string, error)`. Each ledger driver then shrinks to building its envelope and calling runScript. |
| `internal/harness/oracle/oracle.go:40` | medium | (P6) The miss-file name is a contract between packages, and three hand-kept copies hold it. cmd/harness/capture.go:42-45 redeclares missFileName with the comment 'matches internal/harness/oracle's own constant', and freeze.go:68 relies on that copy. repoRoot is copied too, in cmd/harness/freeze.go:104, which says 'matching internal/harness/oracle's own helper'. A comment that says two copies match is the sign that one of them should call the other. | Export one constant from the package that owns the Miss type: `stub.MissFile = "misses.json"`, next to stub.Miss, which stub.go:31 already documents. capture.go and writeMissFile then both use it. That export has real production callers. |
| `internal/harness/oracle/reconcile.go:42` | medium | (P5) The component list is written twice and kept in agreement by hand: once in the Python tuple inside versionProbe (line 25) and once in the pinned slice (lines 42-52). docs/corpus-recapture-chore.md tells maintainers to edit 'both'. reconcile_test.go:31 even pins the silent drift direction as expected behavior. The fakevenv ignores the probe text, so the gate never runs versionProbe at all. | Keep one table, `var pins = []struct{ name string; pin func(*cassette.Manifest) string }{{"beets", func(m *cassette.Manifest) string { return m.Beets }}, ...}`, and pass the names to the probe as argv: `python3 -c 'import json,sys,importlib.metadata as md; print(json.dumps({n: md.version(n) for n in sys.argv[1:]}))' beets unidecode ...`. With one list there is nothing to drift, and the test that pins the drift can go. |
| `internal/harness/oracle/ties.go:120` | medium | runPython repeats the venv branch of shimCommand (oracle.go:318-324: repoRoot, venv/bin/python3, the package's own directory joined to a script name) and the body of runShim (oracle.go:299-307: PYTHONHASHSEED, buffers, Run, stderr in the error). The only differences are the script name and the Shim override. venvVersions (reconcile.go:72-382) repeats the buffer and stderr plumbing a third time. runPython lives in ties.go, yet four files use it, and its doc (ties.go:116-119) still calls it 'the tie and component drivers' counterpart'. | Keep one runner in an untagged file: `func runScript(ctx context.Context, opts Options, script string, args ...string) ([]byte, error)`. runShim becomes `runScript(ctx, opts, "derive_oracle.py", ...)`, and venvVersions reuses the same exec-and-capture helper. |
| `internal/harness/oracle/component.go:15` | low | The Derive* family is inconsistent. DeriveComponentTable carries a 'Table' suffix its siblings lack. DeriveTies returns a decoded analysis.TieEntry, Derive returns *cassette.Expected, and the other four return raw []byte. Options also moves position: (ctx, c, opts) in Derive, DeriveAlbumInfo and DeriveTies, but (ctx, opts, subjects) in DeriveDefectFree and DeriveFileTags. FileTagSubject is singular 'Tag' beside DeriveFileTags and file_tags.go. There is no stutter against the package name (oracle.Options, oracle.Derive read cleanly). | Once these are unexported, pick one shape: `componentTable`, `albumInfo`, `ties`, `defectFree`, `fileTags`, each `(ctx, opts, subject)` returning raw bytes. Leave decoding to the caller that owns the ledger type, as the other four already do. |
| `internal/harness/oracle/defect_free.go:81` | low | DeriveDefectFree builds `filepath.Join(root, "corpus", "cases", s.CaseID)` twice (lines 81 and 87). It restates the layout that caseDir (oracle.go:74) already owns and ignores Options.Dir, which every other case-taking driver honors. | Compute `dir := caseDirFor(root, id)` once per subject through the same helper caseDir uses, and join ResponsePath to that dir. |
| `internal/harness/oracle/oracle.go:139` | low | validateFieldPresent is a verb plus adjective that reads awkwardly, and Derive calls it twice, so raw is decoded three times in total (lines 116, 125, 128). It also checks only that the key is non-empty, despite its name. | `func requireKeys(raw []byte, keys ...string) error`: one probe decode, called once as `requireKeys(raw, "thresholds", "weights")`. |
| `internal/harness/oracle/oracle.go:196` | low | (P7) The error message stutters. writeMissFile already returns 'write miss file: %w' (line 223), and deriveRaw wraps it again with 'write miss file: %w', so a failure reads 'write miss file: write miss file: ...'. Error prefixes across the drivers also disagree in shape: 'oracle: derive %s:', 'oracle: album info %s:', 'oracle: ties %s:', 'oracle: defect free:', 'oracle: derive file tags:'. Some paths return renderProfile and repoRoot errors unwrapped (ties.go:79, album_info.go:64), while their siblings wrap. | Have the caller add context and the callee not repeat it: `err = missErr`, or rename the callee's prefix to 'encode'/'remove'. Settle on one prefix form for every driver, `oracle: <ledger> <case>: ...`, in a single place, which the session helper gives for free. |
| `internal/harness/oracle/oracle.go:211` | low | os.IsNotExist is used on a new code path. Its own doc says new code should use errors.Is(err, fs.ErrNotExist). | `if err := os.Remove(path); err != nil && !errors.Is(err, fs.ErrNotExist)`. |

### internal/harness/stub

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. This is a small, single-concern replay server in one file: it reads every response before it binds a port, matches requests through the corpus's own canonicalizer, refuses to invent a response, and reports both misses and unissued requests. The biggest cost is on the exported surface: Miss carries an on-disk file schema that other packages own, and ServeHTTP is exported only so the package's own http.Server can call it.

6 findings: 0 high, 2 medium, 4 low. Held by patterns: P2 (2), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/stub/stub.go:47` | low | Three field names in Server read against their meaning. `http *http.Server` reuses the package's name, so line 84 (`s.http = &http.Server{...}`) and line 88 (`s.http.Serve`) make the reader work out which `http` is which. `log` (53) reads as a logger, but it holds cassette.Case.Requests. `hit` (57) is singular and reads as a bool, while its sibling `misses` (56) is plural. | Rename to `srv *http.Server`, `requests []cassette.Request`, and `hits map[key]bool` (or `issued`). With a self-describing `requests` name, the comment at lines 50-52 can shrink to one clause. |
| `internal/harness/stub/stub.go:147` | low | `key{method: req.Method, pathQuery: req.PathQuery}` is built from a cassette.Request at line 70 and again at line 147. Miss (35-36) is the same two strings again, written out at line 116. The mapping from a logged request to its match key is a rule, but it is written out at each use. | Add one helper and call it from New and Unused: `func keyOf(req cassette.Request) key { return key{req.Method, req.PathQuery} }`. If Miss moves to cassette per the first finding, consider making key the same comparable type so the miss is just the key that failed to match. |
| `internal/harness/stub/stub_test.go:154` | low | (P9) Unused is a Server method, but its tests are named TestUnused* while every other test is TestServer*. The two Unused tests also repeat the same eight-line load-and-start block (155-164 and 178-187) instead of using a helper like newTestServer. The fakes are real and the expected values are literals written independently of the code, so the tests have no discipline problem beyond this. | Name the tests TestServerUnused... and move the fixture load into a `newFixtureServer(t, dir)` helper beside newTestServer, using t.Cleanup for Close. |

### internal/harness/subsonic/fixture

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. The package loads a committed manifest, checks every seed and path and reports all problems at once, then builds byte-identical tagged FLAC libraries with a digest taken over what actually landed on disk. Its biggest cost is the exported surface: Library.Build is exported but nothing outside the package calls it. After that, doc.go carries oracle facts and plan notes that this code never touches. Comment density is 0.220 (62/282) against testing/fstest at 0.153 (118/771) from the go 1.27.4 GOROOT, and nearly all of the excess is doc.go's second paragraph.

11 findings: 0 high, 2 medium, 9 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (1), P3 (1), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/fixture/collections.go:60` | low | Two digests are built the same way: a sha256 over tab-separated lines written with `_, _ = fmt.Fprintf(h, ...)` under the same 'never returns an error' comment, then hex-encoded with `fmt.Sprintf("%x", h.Sum(nil))` (build.go:58-62, collections.go:64-69). manifest.go:118 hex-encodes with hex.EncodeToString instead. That is two spellings of one operation in a 220-line package. | Pick one encoding, `hex.EncodeToString(h.Sum(nil))`, and use it in all three places. A shared helper is optional at two call sites, but the encoding should match. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/fixture/manifest.go:25` | low | (P6) vorbisCommentBlock = 4 restates tagio's unexported blockVorbisComment (internal/engine/tagio/container.go:23). frontCover = 3 restates a value tagio documents only in prose (picture.go:30, 'front cover at 3'). This is a parallel list across packages that someone must keep in step by hand, and FLAC format constants belong to the package that owns the format. The fixture package is the production caller that would justify exporting them. | Export the two values from tagio, where this package is the named caller (for example `tagio.BlockVorbisComment` and `tagio.PictureFrontCover`), and use them here and in build.go:44. Or give tagio.Container a way to report whether it holds a VORBIS_COMMENT block, so requireVorbisComment does not scan c.Blocks by number. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/fixture/manifest.go:57` | low | Two fields hold a seed name under different names: trackEntry.Seed is the audio seed and trackEntry.Picture is also a seed name. Meanwhile fileEntry, a generic name, is used only for an album cover (line 48). A reader has to work out from check (line 154) that Picture is a seed reference and not image data or a path. | Rename fileEntry to coverEntry. Name the Go field PictureSeed and keep the `json:"picture"` tag, so the manifest format does not change. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/fixture/manifest.go:70` | low | Load's doc promises that 'A manifest with any problem yields one error naming all of them', but line 79-81 returns early for a manifest with no albums, before check runs. A manifest that has no albums and a bad seed reports only the first problem. That is a small contract the code does not keep. | Either move the empty-albums check into check as one more problem (`problems = append(problems, errors.New("manifest declares no albums"))`), or narrow the doc to 'A manifest whose seeds or paths have problems yields one error naming all of them.' |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/fixture/manifest.go:92` | low | A method named check also loads l.seeds, which is the side effect that makes the Library usable. Its doc admits this ('reads the declared seeds from dir into l'), but at the call site in Load (line 83), `lib.check(...)` reads like a pure validation step. | Name it for both jobs, `func (l *Library) loadSeeds(dir string) []error`. Or split reading from checking: `seeds, problems := readSeeds(dir, m.Seeds)` and then `problems = append(problems, checkPaths(m, seeds)...)`. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/fixture/manifest.go:150` | low | `what` already contains the path (`"track " + t.Path`), and checkPath prints the path again, so a duplicate comes out as `track dup.flac: path "dup.flac" is declared twice`. The same doubling happens for covers at line 145. | Make what the kind only (`checkPath("track", t.Path)`), or drop `path %q` from the checkPath messages so the subject appears once. |

### internal/harness/subsonic/grade

Proud: no. Verdict: Sound with nits. The package diffs two servers' recorded answers to one OpenSubsonic session as format-neutral trees, pairs opaque ids in a learn-then-report double walk, and runs a validated divergence ledger over the findings; that mechanism is careful, readable, and table-driven. What costs it most is the exported surface: the whole ledger and finding vocabulary is exported with no production caller, which breaks record 0003, and the package's own assignment.go already shows the right form.

12 findings: 1 high, 5 medium, 6 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (2), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/grade/assignment.go:153` | medium | assignment.problems (153-242) and Row.problems with LoadLedger (ledger.go:139-209) repeat one validation idiom with small differences: a `bad := func(format, args...)` closure, a positional-or-id label, a duplicate-id map, and a `[]struct{ name, value string }` empty-field sweep. The label rule is written three ways. Endpoints compute it before the duplicate check (158-161). Agreements hard-code `agreement %q` in the duplicate message and only reassign label afterwards (202-207). The ledger computes it in LoadLedger (140-143). Inside ledger.go, Row.problems uses bad(...) while activeProblems (239) and Pattern.problems (217) use ps = append(ps, errors.New(...)), so one file mixes two styles. | Choose one form and share it, for example `func label(noun string, i int, id string) string` and `func requireSet(bad func(string, ...any), label string, fields ...[2]string)`. Use those in all three loops, and use the bad closure (or append) consistently within ledger.go. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/grade/differ.go:376` | medium | compareID builds its once-only report keys by joining strings with NUL bytes: `scope + "\x00" + strconv.Itoa(int(space)) + "\x00"` plus "L\x00"+a.lit and similar. The local variable is named at, which shadows the package function at (grade.go:500). The dedupe set (pairing.reported, 70) also lives on the id pairing even though it records reports, not pairs. Go has comparable struct keys for this, and this package already uses one in tableKey (54), so the NUL-joined string is a pattern an agent would copy. | Use `type reportKey struct { scope string; space idSpace; side byte; left, right string }` and `reported map[reportKey]bool`, and give the local a name that does not shadow at. Moving reported into a small set beside differ, instead of on pairing, keeps pairing to its job. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/grade/identity.go:105` | medium | identCtx has three naming problems. Its fields albumArt and albumNm (110-111) abbreviate "album artist" and "album name", and albumArt reads as cover art in a package where coverArt is a compared member (differ.go:36, 224). The Ctx suffix and the parameter name ctx (resolve at 139, resolveFolder at 176, folderPath at 195) borrow the context.Context convention, and grade.go uses ctx for a real context.Context throughout. resolveFolder (176) resolves entityLibrary, not entityFolder, which resolve handles at 158-164, so the name points at the wrong kind. kindOf (64) returns an entityKind in a package where the kind prefix (kindObject and the rest, tree.go:23) belongs to nodeKind, and three different types here are called kind. | Rename the fields to albumArtist and albumTitle, the type to entityScope or enclosing, and the parameter to sc or in. Rename resolveFolder to resolveMusicFolder or resolveLibrary, and kindOf to entityOf. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/grade/assignment.go:169` | low | (P5) Three hand-kept lists restate a const block. The oracle values appear as the const block (32-41), again in problems' membership slice (169), and again in grants' switch (44-54). oracleNavidrome and oracleGonic also repeat the strings of sourceNavidrome and sourceGonic. suppressesOracleTier (ledger.go:388) lists the five oracle-tier kinds by hand against finding.go's const block, so a new differ kind has to be added in two places. | Keep one `var oracles = []oracle{...}` for validation and let grants compare `string(o) == source` outside the both and contract-only cases. Declare `var oracleTierKinds = []Kind{...}` next to the Kind constants and have suppressesOracleTier read it. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/grade/differ.go:137` | low | The same value has two names in the package: differ.endpt, against endpoint in Finding, identCtx, and every other place. The method differ.presenceOnly (324) has the same name as the package var presenceOnly (28). Neither it nor differ.ordered (333) uses its receiver. | Rename endpt to endpoint. Make presenceOnly and ordered plain functions, isPresenceOnly(path) and isOrdered(path), so the var and the predicate stop sharing a name. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/grade/grade.go:523` | low | Report.Format() []byte takes the method name fmt.Formatter reserves for Format(fmt.State, rune). vet's stdmethods lets it pass only because the first argument is not fmt.State. The package also uses Format for the wire format (Finding.Format, Pattern.Format), so r.Format() and f.Format mean different things one line apart. Smaller naming points: graded (264) is a participle used as a type name, and unusedShape (assignment.go:93) breaks the xxxEntry pattern its siblings follow for the not_applicable member. | Rename Format to Bytes() []byte, or implement io.WriterTo. Rename graded to answerPair and unusedShape to notApplicableEntry. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/grade/grade.go:531` | low | Report.Format writes the same block six times: a heading, the sorted keys of a count map, and a `  %s: %d` line per key (531-533, 534-537, 539-542, 543-546, 548-551, 552-555). The copies are each below dupl's threshold, so the gate passes them, but they are still copies. | `func writeCounts[K ~string](b *strings.Builder, heading string, m map[K]int)` and six calls. It covers map[Kind]int too. |

### internal/harness/subsonic/session

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. This is a small, well-judged on-disk format package. Every export has a production caller in cmd/harness/recordoracle.go or internal/harness/subsonic/grade, it has no test seams, and its comment density (0.154) is about half that of archive/tar (0.305). What keeps it short of exemplary is naming: "source" means a name in one field and a record in another, and leakIn returns a half-built value. A few small hand-repeated blocks add to that.

8 findings: 0 high, 1 medium, 7 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/session/session.go:239` | medium | The word "source" means two things in this package. Response.Source (line 96) and the keys of Session.Sources are source names (strings). Addition.Source (line 241) is a Source record, and the source's name sits in Addition.Name. So `Response{Source: "gonic"}` and `Addition{Name: "gonic", Source: Source{...}}` spell the same fact two ways. Join then copies Addition.Name into Response.Source (line 287). An experienced reader stops at that seam, and an agent writing the next record type will copy whichever spelling it saw last. | Use one meaning per word. Either make the name field match the response field, e.g. `type Addition struct { Source string; Meta Source; Resolutions map[string]string; Responses map[string]Response }`, or keep Name and rename Response.Source to Response.SourceName across the package. The first changes less, since grade and recordoracle already read Response.Source as a name. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/session/credentials.go:59` | low | leakIn returns a credentialLeak with an empty path, and scanCredentials sets the path afterward (lines 42-44). So the value is built in two places. It also returns a bool that only repeats len(names) > 0, and that makes the `if len(l.names) > 0` guard in String (line 29) a branch that can never be false. | Have the helper return only what it knows: `func credentialNames(leaf string) []string`. Then the caller builds the whole leak in one place: `if names := credentialNames(s); len(names) > 0 { leaks = append(leaks, credentialLeak{path: path, names: names}) }`. Then delete the guard in String. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/session/credentials.go:117` | low | The two walkers have different shapes. walkJSON takes a decoder and a path prefix, while walkXML takes raw bytes. walkXML's error return does nothing: the function does not recurse, and its only caller throws the error away (`_ = walkXML(...)`, line 48). The `_ =` makes the reader wonder whether an error was dropped on purpose. | Have the walkers share one signature, e.g. `walkXML(body []byte, leaf func(path, s string))` with no return value, and wrap the recursive JSON walker as `walkJSON(body []byte, leaf ...)` so scanCredentials calls both the same way and has nothing to discard. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/session/session.go:160` | low | Each arm of Load's switch repeats the same two statements: append a problem and set `unread[[2]int{i, j}] = true` (lines 162-163, 165-166, 171-172). The anonymous `[2]int` key is also built in Load and read again in validate (line 376), and nothing in the type says which index is which. | Let each arm compute one error, then handle it once: `if err != nil { problems = append(problems, err); unread[ref{i, j}] = true }`, with `type ref struct{ req, resp int }` (or similar) shared by Load and validate. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/session/session.go:296` | low | The package uses two verbs for one act. The exported entry point is CheckRequests and its helper is checkRequests, but the whole-session pass that calls it is validate (line 338), and the docs on Load and Write say "validates". A reader has to work out that "check" and "validate" mean the same thing here. | Choose one verb, e.g. ValidateRequests, validateRequests, and validate, or CheckRequests, checkRequests, and check, and use it in the doc comments too. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/session/session.go:340` | low | The closure `add := func(format string, args ...any) { problems = append(problems, fmt.Errorf(format, args...)) }` is declared twice, here and in checkRequests at line 302. validate then merges checkRequests' slice back into its own. Two collectors written by hand will drift. | Have validate pass its collector in: `func checkRequests(reqs []Request, add func(string, ...any))`, with CheckRequests building the slice it joins. Or declare a named `type problems []error` with an `addf` method that both functions use. |

### internal/harness/subsonic/stack

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. The package runs the two pinned oracles under podman carefully: run-unique labels, secrets passed through the environment instead of argv and redacted from errors, teardown that survives a cancelled context, and validation that reports every problem at once. What costs it most is structure: stack.go does five jobs, one file-local poll loop restates pollUntil, a sentinel is exported only for a test in another package, and a few names are reused or used inconsistently.

11 findings: 0 high, 5 medium, 6 low. Held by patterns: P2 (2), P3 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/stack/stack.go:73` | medium | logTail names both a const (line 73, the string "20") and a method (line 426). Inside the method, line 429 passes `"--tail", logTail`, which reads as recursion or a method value until you remember that methods live in a separate namespace. It compiles, but it is the kind of collision Go Code Review Comments' naming advice exists to prevent. | Rename the const to say what it counts, for example `logTailLines = "20"`, and keep the method name logTail. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/stack/stack.go:289` | medium | The restLibraries bool (line 208) is set by one table member only, and the loop at 289 to 299 ranges over both specs just to find that member. It is an `if` on Navidrome disguised as a generic mechanism, and the reader has to trace the flag to learn that Navidrome alone gets addLibraries. Start also builds the spec table inline (246 to 273), on top of validation, image loading and the three phases, so the table never reads as a table. | Extract `func oracleSpecs(cfg Config, imgs images, secret string) []spec` so the table stands alone. Replace the flag and the loop with a direct call: `if len(cfg.Collections) > 1 { if err := s.addLibraries(ctx, cfg, s.Oracles[0]); err != nil { ... } }`. Stack.Oracles already documents the order as Navidrome, then gonic. If more oracles are expected, use a per-spec hook field `afterRun func(context.Context, *Stack, Config, Oracle) error` instead of a bool. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/stack/stack.go:450` | medium | waitReady writes its own for/select loop on ctx.Done and time.After(pollInterval), the same loop that pollUntil (line 539) already implements and addLibraries uses twice. The only difference is that waitReady builds its error after the deadline, which does not need a second copy of the loop. | Make pollUntil the one implementation and build the error after it returns: `err := pollUntil(ctx, func() bool { ...probe, set folders/finished...; return count == total && folders == want }); if err != nil { switch { case folders >= 0 && folders != want: ...; case finished >= 0: ... }; return fmt.Errorf("%w: %s scan did not finish: %w", errNotReady, o.Name, err) }`. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/stack/images.go:15` | low | One artifact has three names: Config.DigestFile (stack.go:103), type images with loadImages (images.go:20, 29), and errImagesFile, whose message says "bad image digest file". A reader has to work out that these are the same file. Nearby, errNotPinned (images.go:12, a reference with no digest) and errImageNotPinned (stack.go:68, a running image that lacks its pin) differ by one word, and the names do not say which is which. | Pick the field's word and use it everywhere, for example loadDigests and errDigestFile, or rename the field ImagesFile. Name the two pin errors by what failed: errRefNotPinned for the file check and errDigestMismatch for the running-image check. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/stack/stack.go:277` | low | Start copies name, user, password and ref field by field from each spec into an Oracle, and passes s.Oracles[i].container to run (line 281) as a separate argument, even though run already gets sp and runID and could derive the name. spec and Oracle are parallel field lists that have to be kept in agreement by hand. | Give spec a constructor: `func (sp spec) oracle(runID string) Oracle { return Oracle{Name: sp.name, User: sp.user, Password: sp.password, image: sp.ref, container: "dubplate-oracle-" + runID + "-" + sp.name} }`. Have run take that Oracle instead of a loose name string. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/stack/stack.go:409` | low | The method named exec calls exec.CommandContext on line 410, so inside its own body the package name and the method name appear side by side. teardownWait (line 72) also bounds the log fetch in logTail (line 427), which is not teardown. The 5-second probe timeout is a bare literal three times (lines 446, 484, 485). | Rename the method to podman or runPodman (`s.podman(ctx, nil, "rm", ...)` reads as the command line). Give the shared bound a neutral name such as `cleanupWait`, and add `probeTimeout = 5 * time.Second` to the const block. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/stack/stack.go:570` | low | musicFolders and scanStatus have the same body: call c.Do with client.JSON, unmarshal resp.Body into a subsonic-response struct, and wrap the parse error. They differ only in the endpoint and the target struct. cmd/harness/recordoracle.go:448 (probe) writes the same Do-then-Unmarshal helper a third time, and recordoracle.go:405 to 414 declares the same getMusicFolders struct again. | Inside the package, add one helper: `func get(ctx context.Context, c *client.Client, endpoint string, into any) error`, and have musicFolders and scanStatus pass only their structs. Since the module already has two callers, the shared form belongs on client.Client as a decode method that both stack and recordoracle call. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/stack/stack_test.go:743` | low | The failure message reads ndOracle.events directly, without the mutex that every other access to the fake's state takes (snapshot, eventIndex). The httptest handler goroutines write that slice under the lock, so under -race this branch can report a race at the moment it is trying to report a real failure. | Add an events() accessor that clones under f.mu, as snapshot does, and use it in the message. |

### internal/harness/subsonic/xmlrule

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. This is the XML tier of the OpenSubsonic harness. It strips out the names the OpenAPI document adds to the Subsonic XSD, runs the result through a pinned xmllint, and binds each body to what its endpoint declares. The projection mechanism is well judged, the files split by concern, and comment density (0.218) matches encoding/xml (0.217). What costs it most is naming at the exported surface: Source constants differ only by case from unexported types, the word "schema" carries three meanings, and its sibling tier uses different names for the same ideas.

13 findings: 0 high, 4 medium, 9 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (2), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/xmlrule/binding.go:144` | medium | `requiredOf` (binding.go:144) and `properties` (schema.go:227) are the same recursive allOf walk, differing in one statement: one collects `sc.Required`, the other the keys of `sc.Properties`. `admitsOK` (binding.go:119) walks allOf a third time. The copies have already drifted. `properties` guards `part != nil && part.Value != nil`, while `requiredOf` and `admitsOK` guard only `part.Value`, so a nil allOf entry panics in two of the three. Their names also drift: requiredOf, payloadsOf, and attributeOf, but plain properties. | Write one walker, `func allOf(sc *openapi3.Schema) iter.Seq[*openapi3.Schema]`, that yields sc and every resolved allOf part with the full nil guard. Then properties, required, and admitsOK each become a loop over it. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/xmlrule/check.go:16` | medium | The sibling tier conformance has the same Outcome/Violation/Result shape but names its type `Checker`, while this package calls it `Validator` (check.go:80). Conformance reports an undeclared endpoint as `Outcome NoRoute`. This package returns the sentinel error `ErrUnknownEndpoint`, and grade.go:459 immediately turns that error back into a NoRoute finding. The same expected result is a value in one tier and an error in the other, and grade has to translate between them. | An expected result is a value. Add a `NoRoute Outcome = "no-route"` and return `Result{Outcome: NoRoute}` from Check. Name the type `Checker` to match conformance. Keep `ErrNotXML` as an error, since a non-XML body is not an input to this tier. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/xmlrule/check.go:46` | medium | The Source constants `Binding` (check.go:46) and `Schema` (check.go:48) differ only by case from the unexported types `binding` (binding.go:24) and `schema` (schema.go:36). At a call site, `xmlrule.Schema` reads as a type, not a violation source. `Schema` is also the only constant whose string value ("xmllint") does not match its name. An agent copying this enum will reproduce both the collision and the mismatch. | Prefix the constants with their type, as the stdlib does when an enum's member words are generic (http.MethodGet, tar.TypeReg): `SourceDocument`, `SourceBinding`, `SourceXSD`, `SourceResidual`, each with a matching value ("xsd", not "xmllint"). |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/xmlrule/check.go:83` | low | The same file goes by three names: `xsdPath` (check.go:109), `schemaPath` (check.go:83, xmllint.go:88), and `path`. Meanwhile "schema" also names the XSD model type, the OpenAPI `*openapi3.Schema` values (`sc`), and the Source constant. A reader has to work out which schema each use means. | Use "xsd" for everything on the XSD side: `xsdPath` throughout, and keep `schema` for the model type only once the Source constant is renamed. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/xmlrule/check.go:94` | low | New calls Cached(), which resolves CachePath() itself, and then calls CachePath() a second time (check.go:94-101). cmd/harness/fetchxsd.go:38-42 repeats the same double resolution. | Have one unexported `cached() (path string, data []byte, err error)` back both Cached and New. Or have New resolve the path once and read through a `readPinned(path)` helper. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/xmlrule/project.go:137` | low | (P7) `fmt.Errorf("write projection: %w", err)` is written out at five sites in project (project.go:138, 141, 152, 162, and 170). Separately, `residual(ordinal, attr, kind, name, typ)` takes five positional parameters, and the attribute call at project.go:221 passes `name` twice. | Have the encoder writes return a bare error to one wrap site, for example `if err := p.write(enc, tok); err != nil { return nil, fmt.Errorf("write projection: %w", err) }`. Split residual into residualElement(ordinal, name, typ) and residualAttr(ordinal, name, typ). |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/xmlrule/schema.go:16` | low | The package-level `renames` (schema.go:16) is shadowed by the `renames` parameter of newValidator (check.go:109) and of addAdditions (schema.go:194). The only production call (check.go:106) passes the package variable straight back in. Only tests pass a different table (shelfRenames), so inside both functions the name could mean either. | Keep the parameter, since the unit XSD needs its own table, but give the package table a distinct name, `subsonicRenames`, so the parameter no longer shadows anything. |
| `internal/harness/subsonic/xmlrule/schema.go:121` | low | (P7) Error prefixes are inconsistent. Every error from xsd.go and xmllint.go starts with "xmlrule:". Errors from schema.go:121 ("parse schema"), check.go:115, binding.go:44, and project.go do not, and grade wraps them all in "openapi document %s:". The result is mixed chains such as "openapi document x: xmlrule: xmllint version..." next to "openapi document x: parse schema: ...". | Pick one rule. The stdlib puts the package prefix on sentinels only ("xmlrule: ...") and uses plain context in wraps. Apply it to every error the package returns. |

### internal/harness/synth

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The core is good: a pure Build that returns bytes and leaves I/O to its caller, deterministic sha256-derived ids, and one override vocabulary shared by both item sources. The exported surface costs it the most. Facts and its two element types are exported but nobody outside the package reads them, ProjectItems takes a bool that every caller passes as false, and a package named and documented as a synthesizer also holds the reader for captured documents. Overall comment density sits below encoding/csv, but the comments cite process.

12 findings: 2 high, 5 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (1), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/harness/synth/spec.go:1` | high | The package doc says synth 'turns a compact release spec into a synthetic conformance-corpus case' and never mentions ProjectItems or ExtractFacts. Those two read real captured MusicBrainz documents and synthesize nothing. The error prefixes show the strain: library errors read 'synth: seed-items: ...' (seeditems.go:31, 34, 37, 260), which puts a CLI verb name inside a library. An agent adding the next corpus tool will drop it here too, because the name has already stopped meaning anything. The only thing the two halves share is Override and applyOverrides. | Pick one boundary. Either move seeditems.go into its own package (say internal/harness/seed) and export the override step from synth, which then gets a real second caller (`synth.ApplyOverrides`, or a method on a `type Overrides []Override`). Or keep one package, document both halves in the package comment, and rename the package for what both halves share. Library errors should carry the package prefix only ('synth: parse release document: ...') and let cmd/harness add the verb. |
| `internal/harness/synth/release.go:206` | medium | `int(tr.Length * 1000)` appears twice (206 and 211), and it truncates rather than rounds. I checked it: 5,907 of the 600,000 millisecond values below 10 minutes come out short, for example 1.001 s renders as 1000 ms. The document's track length then disagrees with the item's `length` from buildItems, and ProjectItems on a built document reads back a different value. The inverse, `float64(tr.Length) / 1000`, lives in a third place (seeditems.go:57). | Use one helper for each direction: `func millis(sec float64) int { return int(math.Round(sec * 1000)) }`, called at both sites. |
| `internal/harness/synth/seeditems.go:104` | medium | The package models one wire format, WS/2, under two prefixes. The encode side is named for the format (wsRelease, wsTrack, wsArtistCredit in release.go:29-167). The decode side is named for provenance (capturedRelease, capturedTrack, capturedCredit at 110-179). The provenance name is wrong: the code says it also decodes documents this package built (flexPosition 77-82, capturedTrack 162-165, trackArtist 202-205). Inside one package, the same concept carries two names that suggest a difference that isn't there. | Name both sides for the format and the direction. Keep `wsRelease` for the rendered shape and name the decode shape for its role, for example `releaseDoc`, `docTrack`, `docCredit`. Or, where the field sets overlap, decode into the ws types directly, with flexPosition as the Position type on both. |
| `internal/harness/synth/synth.go:62` | medium | buildItems (synth.go:62-88) and ProjectItems (seeditems.go:42-72) are the same loop: the same eight-key ItemFields literal, the same `comp` rule, the same `multiDisc` and `disc = mi + 1` rule. They differ only in where the values come from, plus ProjectItems' mb_trackid. The item shape is two parallel lists that have to be kept in agreement by hand. mbAlbumIDField (spec.go:42) exists, yet both literals spell "mb_albumid" inline (synth.go:78, seeditems.go:58). | Make one helper the implementation and have both loops call it. For example, `type trackRow struct{ album, albumArtist, artist, title, albumID string; length float64; track, year, disc int; comp bool }` with `func (r trackRow) item() cassette.ItemFields`, where disc == 0 means single-medium. buildItems and ProjectItems then only fill rows, and the item keys live in one place. |
| `internal/harness/synth/release.go:229` | low | The composite literals in buildRelease and singleArtistCredit spell out zero values: `ASIN: nil, Barcode: nil, Packaging: nil` (234-239), `Title: ""`, `ArtistCredit: nil`, `Disambiguation: ""`, and `Video: false` (205-213, 223, 251), plus `JoinPhrase: ""` (57). None of the JSON tags uses omitempty, so every key is emitted anyway, and the explicit zeros carry nothing the struct definition does not. Agents will copy this as house style. | Omit zero-valued fields from the literals. Where emitting a null or empty key is the point, the struct's tag already guarantees it, and the type's doc comment is the place to say so once. |
| `internal/harness/synth/seeditems.go:241` | low | Three sibling types use three naming schemes: Facts, FactAlias (prefixed with the parent's name, so it stutters as synth.FactAlias next to synth.Facts), and ArtistAliases (no prefix, plural). releaseIsVariousArtists (198) takes a credit slice, not a release. convertAliases (291) says how it works, not what it returns. | Use `alias`, `artistAliases`, `facts` (unexported per the surface finding), `isVariousArtists(credits)`, and `toAliases`, or simply inline the one-line FactAlias(a) conversion. |
| `internal/harness/synth/spec.go:30` | low | (P8) The override operations are untyped string constants, and Override.Op is a plain string (135). Nothing ties the field to its domain, so an agent writing the next enum-valued JSON field will copy the bare-string form. OpMissing and OpExtra also have no reference outside the package. | `type Op string` with `const (OpField Op = "field"; OpMissing Op = "missing"; OpExtra Op = "extra")` and `Op Op` on Override. encoding/json decodes a named string type unchanged, so the spec format does not move. |
| `internal/harness/synth/synth.go:130` | low | The Index range check and its error text are copied verbatim for OpField (130-132) and OpMissing (135-137). The track artist fallback `artist := spec.Artist; if tr.ArtistCredit != "" {...}` is written twice (synth.go:66-69, release.go:194-197). The decode-and-wrap of a release document is written twice (seeditems.go:29-32, 258-261), and only ProjectItems validates id and media afterward. | Check the range once before the switch (`if ov.Op != OpExtra && (ov.Index < 0 \|\| ov.Index >= len(items))`). Add `func (t Track) artist(release string) string` and `func decodeRelease(doc []byte) (*capturedRelease, error)`, and have both callers use them. |

### internal/id

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. The package is a 27-line leaf value type in the shape of netip.Addr and uuid.UUID, and every exported symbol has a production caller: New and Parse have many, IsZero has submission.go:113 and admin.go:108, String has lease.go:49, Value is reached through database/sql at apply.go:120, and ID has identity.go:202. Its one real cost is on the surface axis. ID implements driver.Valuer but not sql.Scanner, so the read half of the store boundary has been written by hand three times in internal/auth. Density is 14/27 = 0.52 against net/netip at 428/1093 = 0.39. That excess comes from the file being small: every comment is a doc comment on an exported symbol or the package clause, and each one gives a reason.

4 findings: 1 high, 1 medium, 2 low. Held by patterns: P2 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/id/id.go:34` | low | Parse rejects uppercase by decoding into a fresh slice and then re-encoding it to compare against s. That costs two allocations and asks the reader to see that the round trip is an uppercase check. The length check on line 31 already guarantees the size, so the decode can write straight into the array. | var id ID for i := 0; i < len(s); i++ { 	if c := s[i]; !('0' <= c && c <= '9' \|\| 'a' <= c && c <= 'f') { 		return ID{}, errSyntax 	} } hex.Decode(id[:], []byte(s)) return id, nil |
| `internal/id/id_test.go:8` | low | TestID only exercises New, while its siblings are named for the function they test (TestParse, TestIsZero, TestString, TestValue). The test discipline is otherwise clean: expectations are literals rather than values computed by the code under test, and there are no seams. The package names themselves are clean too: id.ID follows the uuid.UUID and time.Time precedent and does not stutter. | Rename it TestNew. |

### internal/identity

Proud: no. Verdict: Sound with nits, short of exemplary. This is a small leaf package. The address normalizer is careful in ways a linter cannot judge: it drops net/mail's error text, checks that the result parses back to itself, and builds Default_Ignorable_Code_Point from the stdlib's own tables. The axis that costs it most is the exported surface: `type UserID id.ID` throws away id.ID's String, IsZero and Value methods, and that loss is already hand-patched at about fifteen call sites across auth, ops and intake. Two Identity fields have no production reader, and the comment density is more than five times net/mail's.

11 findings: 1 high, 6 medium, 4 low. Held by patterns: P1 (2), P2 (4), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/identity/identity.go:65` | medium | NormalizeAddress scans the string twice: strings.ContainsFunc finds an invisible rune, then invisibleRune (lines 135-142) walks it again to find the same rune. invisibleRune therefore has an unreachable `return ""` that needs a six-line comment (129-134) to explain. It also formats the rune with `fmt.Sprintf("U+%04X", r)`, which re-implements fmt's `%U` verb. Its name says it returns a rune, but it returns a formatted string. | Use one pass and the stdlib verb: `if i := strings.IndexFunc(normalized, IsInvisibleRune); i >= 0 { r, _ := utf8.DecodeRuneInString(normalized[i:]); return "", fmt.Errorf("%w: carries a control or invisible character (%U)", ErrInvalidAddress, r) }`. Then delete invisibleRune and its comment. |
| `internal/identity/identity.go:232` | medium | The receiver `id` of Identity.Valid shadows the package `id` imported at line 19, inside the same file that declares `type UserID id.ID`. It compiles only because Valid never needs the package. The first edit that does need it will fail to compile with a confusing error. The test file copies the name (`id := Identity{...}` at identity_test.go:454 and :485), and ops.NetworkActor (ops.go:54) names its parameter `id identity.Identity` in a package that also imports internal/id. | Use a short receiver name taken from the type that does not collide with an import, for example `func (i Identity) Valid() bool`. Use the same name, `i` or `ident`, for local variables of this type. |
| `internal/identity/identity.go:217` | low | The field is named Principal but documented as "the member's username", and its only writer fills it from `member.Username` (auth/seam.go:135). The same value is called Username in the roster, users table and ops request, and Principal in this field, the `principal` log key (api.go:48) and the duplicate_principal code. A reader has to learn that the two words mean the same thing. | Name the field Username to match the roster it is read from, or rename the roster side to match. Either way, use one word for one value across the boundary. |

### internal/intake

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. Intake uses the store as its work queue, wakes workers through a one-slot channel, recovers from panics on member bytes, and guards the tus path carefully, and that machinery is better than most code. What costs it most is a boundary built around the code rather than for the caller: there are five exported readers whose only job is to be passed back into the package's own Config, an exported Route API with no caller outside the package, a duplicated Limits/limits pair, and ids carried as raw []byte, which goes against record 0012.

15 findings: 4 high, 8 medium, 3 low. Held by patterns: P1 (2), P2 (1), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/intake/seam.go:23` | high | Limits (seam.go:23-29) and limits (limits.go:21-27) hold the same four fields, and Limits.limits() (seam.go:31-38) copies one into the other field by field. They have to be kept in agreement by hand. The internal code mixes them: judgeZIP takes the exported Limits (worker.go:235) and converts it at worker.go:250, judgeFiles takes gate.Policy, and create and reserveExtracted take limits. An agent copying this will mirror every exported struct with an unexported one. | Keep one struct. Once the round trip above is gone, unexport it as `limits` and delete the converter. If it stays exported, delete the unexported twin and pass Limits everywhere. |
| `internal/intake/seam.go:350` | high | Submission, file and collection ids travel as []byte throughout the package: parseID returns v[:] (submission.go:111-118), minted[:] appears at submission.go:194 and worker.go:263, and identity.UserID(uploader) converts a scanned []byte by slice-to-array conversion (limits.go:180), which panics on a length mismatch. Because the type is lost, hex.EncodeToString restates id.ID.String nine times (seam.go:350, worker.go:143 and 148, cleanup.go:176, 194 and 224, startup.go:97 and 164, receipt.go:36, tus.go:139). Record 0012 says an id is the type in internal/id or a named type built on it. Every local variable named `id` also shadows the imported id package, so the next agent who adds id.New() inside move or check gets a compile error and renames the import. | Carry id.ID, or named types like `type submissionID id.ID`, in every struct field and parameter. Give id.ID a Scan method in internal/id so rows scan into it. wireID, extractDir and uploadName then take id.ID and call .String(), and parseID returns (id.ID, bool). |
| `internal/intake/cleanup.go:116` | medium | The staged-set rule, meaning which bytes an open submission keeps, exists in three forms that must agree. One is the SQL in stagedSet (startup.go:63-69). Another is the Go switch in uploadGoes (cleanup.go:116-136). The third is the Go and SQL pair in cleanExtraction (cleanup.go:168-203), which repeats 'accepted separate-tracks failing members'. clean's doc (cleanup.go:25) defines cleanup as 'the bytes outside every submission's staged set', but no shared code expresses that relationship. | Choose one definition, for example a predicate `func kept(st state, unit gate.Unit, r role, failed bool) bool` that uploadGoes and cleanExtraction both call. Then add one test that runs stagedSet against the predicate over every state, unit and role combination, so the SQL form cannot drift. |
| `internal/intake/config.go:401` | medium | ResolveStaging resolves nothing. It refuses overlapping or relative paths, and its own doc says it 'never resolves symlinks' (config.go:393-396). Callers at main.go:128 and doctor.go:42 read it as returning a resolved path. FreeSpaceFloor (381) returns a reader func like WindowsFrom and LimitsFrom but is named like SweepInterval, which reads once, so the package has two naming schemes for one shape. | Rename it CheckStaging, or ValidateStaging. If the readers stay exported, keep one naming scheme: FreeSpaceFloorFrom alongside WindowsFrom and LimitsFrom. |
| `internal/intake/seam.go:340` | medium | A collection's admission_format_allowlist is read and narrowed three ways. seam.go:340 runs the same SELECT as effectiveAllowlist (limits.go:207) and then calls narrow, readChecking reads it through a join (worker.go:190), and judge narrows it again (worker.go:179). The two identical queries differ only in running on a *store.Tx versus a *store.Handle. | Have effectiveAllowlist take a small querier interface (`interface{ QueryRow(ctx, q string, args ...any) *sql.Row }`, which both Tx and Handle satisfy) and call it from reasonReader.read. |
| `internal/intake/submission.go:52` | medium | (P5) The journey's vocabulary is written out by hand in several places. openSubmission (52) restates state.terminal() (41-43) as a SQL literal. stagedSet (startup.go:63-69), unreceivedUploads (startup.go:134) and checkSpace (limits.go:129-131) hardcode 'uploading', 'checking', 'matching' and 'accepted', while other queries bind stateUploading as a parameter (limits.go:79, sweep.go:79). Container values appear as 'zip' and 'files' in SQL even though gate.ContainerZip exists. The file role has no type at all: "archive", "audio" and "extra" are bare strings at submission.go:225-229, accept.go:63-65, worker.go:216 and cleanup.go:132. | Add `type role string` with roleArchive, roleAudio and roleExtra beside state, and bind state, role and container values as query parameters everywhere. Derive the terminal set once, for example `var terminalStates = []state{stateRejected, stateCancelled, stateExpired}`, with terminal() as slices.Contains over it. |
| `internal/intake/submission.go:120` | medium | The struct type newSubmission (120) sits next to the constructor newSubmissions (81), and the two names differ by one letter. In Go, `newX` names a constructor, so a type with that name reads as a function at every use (seam.go:71, rejectcodes_test.go). The past-participle type names `created` (129) and `checked` (worker.go:152) also read like booleans. | Rename the types to createRequest (or submissionRequest), createdSubmission and checkingSubmission. |
| `internal/intake/tus.go:29` | medium | maxUploadBytes = 17179869184 repeats maxAlbumBytesSetting.Ceiling "17179869184" (config.go:56), and its doc says it is that ceiling. Nothing ties the two together: no test references both. Raising the ceiling would leave tusd answering 413 to an album the setting admits. | Declare the number once and derive the string: `const maxAlbumBytesCeiling = 16 << 30` and `Ceiling: strconv.Itoa(maxAlbumBytesCeiling)` in the var initializer, with tusd's MaxSize set from the same const. The setting Docs that restate Floor, Ceiling and Default (config.go:53, 67, ...) carry the same risk 15 times; they could be built with fmt.Sprintf from the same consts. |
| `internal/intake/limits.go:31` | low | Go Code Review Comments names error types FooError, but limitRefusal (limits.go:31) and manifestRefusal (submission.go:141) break that convention. The field Windows.ResumeWindow (intake.go:55) repeats its type's name, and it sits beside Hold and StallAfter, which do not. | Rename them limitError and manifestError, and rename Windows.ResumeWindow to Windows.Resume. |
| `internal/intake/worker.go:73` | low | The one-column scan closure `func(rows *sql.Rows) ([]byte, error) { var id []byte; return id, rows.Scan(&id) }` appears four times (worker.go:73-76, sweep.go:112-114, cleanup.go:185-187 and 208-210). startup.go:106 inlines strings.ToValidUTF8(..., "�") instead of calling validUTF8 (seam.go:353). The literal "tus" directory name is joined by hand in six places (receipt.go:35, tus.go:74, cleanup.go:93 and 155, startup.go:165, worker.go:148). | Declare `func scanID(rows *sql.Rows) ([]byte, error)` once (or scan into id.ID after the id finding), call validUTF8 at startup.go:106, and use `const tusDir = "tus"` beside extractDir and uploadName. |

### internal/intake/gate

Proud: no. Verdict: Sound with nits. The checks themselves are expert work: decode failures are classified by the gate's own I/O, the ZIP central directory is counted in constant memory before archive/zip allocates, and every exported symbol has a production caller in internal/intake. What keeps it under the owner's bar is inconsistency at the package's edges: two error contracts, two types for one kind of path backed by a reason that is false in Go, two meanings of "audio", and a decode parameter that exists only for tests.

12 findings: 2 high, 5 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (1), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/intake/gate/path.go:16` | high | ValidPath's doc says p is []byte "because a stored path need not be valid UTF-8". In Go that is false: a string holds arbitrary bytes. The package contradicts itself here, since ManifestFile.Path (manifest.go:22-25) is a string documented as "need not be valid UTF-8", and StagedFile.Path is a string too. Meanwhile FileReason.Path, ZIPMember.Path, ZIPWalk.Extras ([][]byte) and ZIPRefusal.Path are []byte. The result is conversions in both directions: ValidPath([]byte(name)) at manifest.go:116 and zip.go:128, []byte(f.Name) at zip.go:144/153/277, and string(m.Path) at intake/worker.go:275,276,289. CheckAlbum takes extras as []string while ZIPWalk.Extras is [][]byte. The real reason for []byte is probably BLOB binding in the store, which is a store concern leaking into a package whose doc says it imports nothing from the store. An agent will copy the false rationale. | Use one representation, string, as archive/zip's File.Name does: `func ValidPath(p string) bool`, `Path string` on FileReason, ZIPMember and ZIPRefusal, and `Extras []string`. Convert to []byte once, where intake binds the BLOB. Delete the UTF-8 sentence. |
| `internal/intake/gate/zip.go:269` | high | The package has two opposite error contracts. gate.go:26 declares ErrCouldNotRun as the mark of a check the server failed to complete, and CheckFile follows it (couldNotRun at file.go:218). WalkZIP and Extract do not: a read, open, stat, write or sync failure comes back bare (zip.go:74, 79, 187, 211, 236, 271), and the archive's fault is instead typed as *ZIPRefusal. intake/worker.go:301 has to add ErrCouldNotRun itself. A third check written next to either one copies whichever it lands beside, and each caller must know which convention each entry point uses. | Give both entry points one contract. archiveFault returns couldNotRun("read staged archive", src.err), and WalkZIP and Extract wrap their own server faults the same way, leaving ctx.Err() bare as CheckFile does. zipFault in the worker then passes errors through unchanged. |
| `internal/intake/gate/zip.go:22` | medium | *ZIPRefusal is an error type without the Error suffix the standard library uses throughout (fs.PathError, strconv.NumError, json.SyntaxError). It also sits beside Refusal (manifest.go:31), which is not an error, so two "refusal" types have different kinds. ZIPWalk (zip.go:52) names an action rather than the thing it is, which is a vetted, open archive with Members, Extract and Close, the role zip.ReadCloser plays for zip.OpenReader. | `type ZIPError struct{ Reason reject.Reason; Path string; cause error }`. Consider `type Archive` returned by `OpenZIP`, or keep WalkZIP as the verb and name the result after the noun. |
| `internal/intake/gate/zip.go:71` | medium | WalkZIP takes `separateTracks bool`, but the package has a Unit type for exactly this and CheckFile takes `unit Unit`. The caller has to write `sub.unit == gate.UnitTrack` (intake/worker.go:237), and a bare `true` at a call site does not say what it means. | `func WalkZIP(root *os.Root, name string, p Policy, unit Unit) (*ZIPWalk, error)`, branching on `unit == UnitTrack` inside, as CheckFile does with UnitAlbum. |
| `internal/intake/gate/zip.go:142` | medium | `_, isAudio := p.admitted(extension(name))` and the `audio` flag at zip.go:147 use "audio" to mean "an allowlisted format". The exported IsAudio (policy.go:63) and CheckManifest's audio count (manifest.go:79) use it to mean "any music extension". So no-audio means one thing for loose files and another for a ZIP, and a separate-tracks ZIP drops an MP3 as an extra where a separate-tracks manifest treats it as audio. A local named after an exported predicate with a different meaning is the kind of name that misleads the next reader. | Name the local for what it is (`row, kept := p.admitted(ext)`). If no-audio is meant to mean the same thing in both containers, call isAudioExt in one of the two places so the package has one definition. |
| `internal/intake/gate/file.go:375` | low | Three wrappers keep "the first error": recorder (file.go:375), recordedReaderAt (zip.go:283) and recordedWriter (zip.go:303). Their names are inconsistent (recorder vs recorded*), and so are their EOF tests (errors.Is(err, io.EOF) at file.go:384 vs err != io.EOF at zip.go:295). recorder.fault and archiveFault are the same classifier with two outputs. Separate types are justified because the interfaces differ, but the family should read as one. | Rename recorder to recordedReader, use `err != io.EOF` in all three (io documents that Read returns io.EOF itself), and keep each fault classifier beside its reader. |
| `internal/intake/gate/policy.go:58` | low | audioExtensions lists ".flac" even though its comment says it adds the extensions the format table does not have, and isAudioExt (policy.go:68) also checks the table. The two lists must be kept in agreement by hand. | Remove ".flac" from audioExtensions so it holds only the music formats the library does not keep, as its comment says. |
| `internal/intake/gate/policy.go:63` | low | IsAudio's parameter `path` shadows the imported path package that the same file uses in extension (policy.go:87). | `func IsAudio(name string) bool`, matching extension(name). |

### internal/intake/staging

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The package is a small fail-closed check that one rename can move a file from staging into the library. Its file split and its path-nesting logic are exemplary. Its weak point is the exported surface: Dir hands out raw statx facts (Dev, Mask, MountID, StatxErr), so doctor.go re-implements the same-mount rule against the Linux-only unix.STATX_MNT_ID in a file with no build tag, which undoes the portability that mntIDMask exists to protect.

8 findings: 1 high, 2 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P2 (3), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/intake/staging/staging.go:6` | medium | The package is named for one of its inputs, not for what it provides. Its callers stat the library root (serve.go:167) and every collection root (doctor.go:50) through `staging.Read`, and the space query is filesystem-wide. The name also takes the natural variable name, so the caller has to write `stagingDir, library := staging.Read(...), staging.Read(...)` (doctor.go:30). Go Code Review Comments warns against package names that steal good variable names. `Read` is also an odd verb for an operation that stats a directory and returns metadata, since `Read` in Go suggests io.Reader. | Name the package for the concern, for example `package volume` (doctor already calls the row "music volume") or `mount`, with `volume.Stat(path) Dir`, `volume.Check(staging, library)`, and `volume.FreeBytes(dir)`. Callers can then write `staging, library := volume.Stat(...), volume.Stat(...)`. |
| `internal/intake/staging/staging.go:67` | low | The role labels "staging directory" and "library root" are written out at lines 53, 56, and 70, and again in doctor.go:35. dirErr's "%s %s does not exist" wording is repeated by hand at doctor.go:37. Two lists of the same role pairs have to be kept in agreement by hand. | After the mount rule moves into SameMount, the statx loop at 67-77 goes away. Keep the role labels in one place, either as unexported consts or by passing them once to a single helper that dirErr and SameMount both use. |
| `internal/intake/staging/staging.go:79` | low | The refusal messages switch register. Lines 60 and 64 describe the state ("nest", "sit on different devices"), while line 79 prescribes ("must sit in one mount"). A reader comparing doctor output sees two voices for the same kind of refusal. | Use one register for every refusal, for example "... sit in different mounts", to match line 64. |
| `internal/intake/staging/staging_linux.go:53` | low | (P6) The free-space formula `st.Bavail * uint64(st.Frsize)` also appears at internal/store/inspect.go:359, behind store's own statfs seam. The two packages each own a copy of one fact about statfs. | Keep one implementation in a leaf package that both can import, so store does not reach up into intake. For example, move FreeBytes into the renamed volume package placed outside intake and have store call it. |

### internal/notify

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The package is small, with a clean Config/New/Mailer/Send surface that fails closed on TLS and keeps recipient addresses out of its errors, and that judgment deserves credit. Two things fall below the bar, and agents would copy both: four exported sentinels that no code outside the package reads, against record 0003, and two struct fields that exist only so tests can steer Send. On top of that the comments run at nearly twice net/smtp's density, mostly rationale for those seams and a package doc that names callers that do not exist.

10 findings: 2 high, 3 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P1 (2), P2 (1), P4 (4).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/notify/notify.go:93` | medium | Send runs three CR/LF guards (93-101), and then msg.From and msg.To parse the same values through net/mail. A scratch run confirmed that net/mail.ParseAddress already rejects CR and LF in an address, so only the subject guard does any work. The From address is also checked at load (auth/config.go:410-419), never changes for the life of a Mailer, and is still re-guarded and re-parsed on every Send. One fact is validated in three places. | Validate From once in New: `func New(cfg Config) (*Mailer, error)`. newMailer in serve.go:404 already returns an error. Send then guards only the subject and lets msg.To reject a bad recipient: `if strings.ContainsAny(subject, "\r\n") { return errHeaderInjection }`. |
| `internal/notify/notify.go:33` | low | ErrInvalidFrom names a header and ErrInvalidRecipient names a role, and the guard labels at 93-99 ('recipient', 'subject', 'from') mix the same two vocabularies. The log message 'notify sent' (150) uses the package name as a verb. Config, Mailer, New and Send are clean and do not stutter (notify.Mailer, notify.New). | Use one vocabulary throughout, From/To or Sender/Recipient, for example errInvalidFrom and errInvalidTo with labels "from" and "to". |
| `internal/notify/notify.go:145` | low | The non-SendError branch formats err with %v, which cuts the error chain. A context that is cancelled before the dial returns context.Canceled (90-92), but one cancelled mid-send comes back as an ErrSend that errors.Is(err, context.Canceled) does not match. The same cause gets two different error identities depending on timing. | This branch carries no recipient list, so it can wrap both: `fmt.Errorf("%w: %s:%d: %w", errSend, host, port, err)`. |

### internal/opensubsonic

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. This directory is the root of the OpenSubsonic protocol layer, which is meant to ship later as its own module (BACKLOG #96). It holds only a boundary test: it runs `go list -deps -test ./...`, fails on any dubplate path outside the layer, tests its own parser with literal expectations, and refuses to pass if the listing never names the client. What costs it most is naming and shape: the package has no doc comment, so the layer's one rule lives only in a test failure message, and the test names say less than the sibling tests in harness/conform do. Comment density is 0.050 on the test file (no non-test files) against go/build/deps_test.go at 0.031, which is no excess.

5 findings: 0 high, 0 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P3 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/opensubsonic/boundary_test.go:19` | low | (P6) The body of `violations` (:19-32) repeats internal/harness/conform/arch_test.go:301-313 `serverLinkViolations` statement for statement: cut each line at the first space, then match the path exactly or by prefix plus a slash. Here the copy is correct, because a shared helper elsewhere in internal/ would be exactly the import this test forbids. Nothing records that, so the next agent to tidy the module could pull both into one helper and break the boundary the test exists to hold. | Keep the copy. Add one clause to the doc comment at :16-18 giving the reason the code cannot show: `The layer keeps its own copy of this parser, since it may import no helper from the rest of the module.` |
| `internal/opensubsonic/boundary_test.go:34` | low | `violations` and `TestViolations` are generic names. The one sibling with the same shape names what it checks and what the test proves: `serverLinkViolations` and `TestServerLinkCheckFiresOnItsOwnListing` (internal/harness/conform/arch_test.go:301, :336). TestViolations proves the check fires on canned listings, but its name only says it exercises a function. Agents copy the nearest example, so two conventions for one idiom in one repo will spread. | Use `boundaryViolations` and `TestBoundaryCheckFiresOnItsOwnListing`, matching the conform pair. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/boundary_test.go:40` | low | The case table packs each listing into one expression on one line, 134 to 162 characters wide (:40, :42-45). The first case joins five fragments, so the test-variant lines it exists to exercise are hard to see. | Put each case on several lines with named fields (`name:`, `listing:`, `want:`), and build each listing from its lines with `strings.Join([]string{"net/http", layerRoot + "/client", layerRoot + "/client [" + layerRoot + "/client.test]"}, "\n")` so each listed package reads as one row. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/boundary_test.go:57` | low | (P9) `TestLayerImportsNothingInternal` claims more than it checks. The layer imports its own internal paths (internal/opensubsonic/client and conformance) and :27 deliberately allows them. The failure message at :66 states the rule correctly ("outside internal/opensubsonic"), so the name and the message disagree. | Name it after the rule as the message states it: `TestLayerImportsNothingOutsideItself`, or `TestBoundary` to match the file name. |

### internal/opensubsonic/client

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. This is a small, careful OpenSubsonic client. Do returns the verbatim answer on every server reply, strips the credential-bearing URL from transport errors, and makes the zero value useful in the same way http.Client does. Most of the cost is the package name: `client` labels a role, so five harness packages that never send a request import `client.Format` and `client.XML` to name protocol vocabulary. A few exported envelope fields and unexported error types exist for no reader outside tests. Comment density is 39/182 = 0.214 against net/smtp at 131/367 = 0.357 (net/rpc/jsonrpc is 0.200), so comments are lean and not a problem.

7 findings: 1 high, 2 medium, 4 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/opensubsonic/client/client.go:7` | high | `package client` is named for a role, not for the protocol, but it also holds the protocol's vocabulary (Format, JSON, XML, Envelope, ParseEnvelope, Error). grade/tree.go:125, grade/differ.go:138, grade/finding.go:52, grade/ledger.go:357 and session/credentials.go:39 import it only to say `client.Format` or `client.XML`, which reads as 'the client's XML' in code that never makes a request. The name also collides with the variable name callers would naturally use, so recordoracle.go:89 declares a field `client *client.Client`. Google's style guide warns against package names that callers' locals will shadow, and against names that say nothing about the content. When the built-in OpenSubsonic streamer needs Format and the error envelope, it will have to import a package called `client` or duplicate the types. Agents will copy the role-named pattern into new code. | Name the package for the protocol, the way net/smtp exports smtp.Client. Make internal/opensubsonic itself `package opensubsonic`, or a `subsonic` package, holding Client, Do, Format, Envelope, ParseEnvelope and Error. Call sites then read `opensubsonic.JSON`, `opensubsonic.ParseEnvelope(body, f)`, `*opensubsonic.Error`, `&opensubsonic.Client{...}`. The boundary test at internal/opensubsonic/boundary_test.go already treats that directory as the layer root. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/client/client.go:190` | medium | jsonEnvelope and xmlEnvelope repeat the same three fields (Status, ServerVersion, Error). Two struct literals (lines 222 and 229) then copy those fields into Envelope. wireError (line 185) already shows that one struct can carry both tag sets, so the envelope ignores the package's own pattern. The result is two parallel lists that must be edited together when a field is added. | Use one wire type with both tags, `type wireEnvelope struct { XMLName xml.Name `json:"-" xml:"subsonic-response"`; Status string `json:"status" xml:"status,attr"`; ServerVersion string `json:"serverVersion" xml:"serverVersion,attr"`; Error *wireError `json:"error" xml:"error"` }`. The JSON branch decodes `struct{ R *wireEnvelope `json:"subsonic-response"` }`, the XML branch decodes a wireEnvelope directly, and a single `env := Envelope{...}` follows the switch. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/client/client.go:38` | low | The set of formats is written twice: once in Valid (`f == JSON \|\| f == XML`) and again in ParseEnvelope's switch, whose default branch at line 232 rejects the same values. A third format would need both edits, and the default branch is unreachable from Do, which has already checked Valid at line 122. | Keep the switch as the place that decides, since ParseEnvelope is exported and must reject unknown formats itself. Valid can stay as the cheap pre-send check, but its doc should say that the list is mirrored in ParseEnvelope. Alternatively, drop the second check and let ParseEnvelope call `if !format.Valid()` before the switch, so both sites share one list. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/client/client.go:49` | low | Client.Name is the client identifier sent as `c`, but at the only construction site (stack.go:181-184) it sits next to `o.Name`, which is the oracle server's name. On a Client, a bare Name could mean the client or the server it talks to, and only the doc comment settles which. | Name the field after its meaning, `ClientID string // sent as the c parameter`, or after the protocol's own word. If the package is renamed (first finding), `opensubsonic.Client{ClientName: ...}` no longer stutters against the package. |

### internal/opensubsonic/conformance

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike, close to sound. The surface is small and every export has a caller. The naming avoids stutter, the comments are lean (0.271 against net/url's 0.364), and the oneOf branch filtering shows real judgment. The cost is the core mechanism: Check repeats most of kin-openapi's ValidateResponse by hand so it can catch that function's silent passes, then calls the same function, which repeats every step again. It also keeps its own copy of a skip list the library marks as incomplete.

8 findings: 1 high, 3 medium, 4 low. Held by patterns: P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/opensubsonic/conformance/conformance.go:93` | high | Lines 93-118 repeat, step by step, the start of kin-openapi v0.153.0 ValidateResponse (openapi3filter/validate_response.go:23-118): the HEAD skip, the 301/304/307/308 skip list, the Status-then-Default lookup, the empty-content check, the Content.Get call, and the nil-schema check. Lines 127-143 then build a ResponseValidationInput and call ValidateResponse, which runs every one of those steps again. It also decodes the body a second time (with UseNumber) and validates response headers, which this caller always fills with Content-Type alone. The only thing that call adds is Schema.Value.VisitJSON(value, MultiErrors(), VisitAsResponse()). neverValidated (167-175) copies a list whose upstream comment reads "TODO: The list is probably missing some." When kin-openapi adds a status to that list, the checker reports a skipped body as Valid. That silent pass is exactly the defect this package exists to prevent. An experienced developer would object to wrapping a high-level call whose every early exit has to be guessed in advance, when the library exposes the primitive underneath it. An agent would copy the pattern: mirror the library's guards, then call it anyway. | Once media.Schema is found and the body is decoded, validate directly: `err := media.Schema.Value.VisitJSON(decoded, openapi3.MultiErrors(), openapi3.VisitAsResponse())`, then `collect(err, claimed, &out)`. Decode with UseNumber to match kin-openapi's number handling, and keep `openapi3.EnableJSONSchema2020()` on the Checker for a 3.1 document. This deletes neverValidated, the ResponseValidationInput and RequestValidationInput, the ResponseError unwrap, the second decode, and the ctx parameter. HEAD stays only if the checker decides on its own that HEAD has no body to check. The package doc's second paragraph then shrinks to one sentence. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/conformance/conformance.go:51` | medium | NoSchema names one cause of the outcome, not the outcome itself. Its own doc (48-50) and line 93 also use it for a HEAD request and for statuses kin-openapi never validates, where a schema may well exist. The string is rendered verbatim as a finding value (grade.go:434), so a 304 answer is reported as "no-schema". The XML sibling names the same class Unvalidated (xmlrule/check.go:34). | Name the class: `Unvalidated Outcome = "unvalidated"`. That matches the doc's own wording ("nothing was validated") and the sibling package. Finding 1 would remove most of the non-schema causes anyway. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/conformance/conformance.go:181` | medium | In Check (92), `status` is the HTTP status int. In collect (181) and pickBranches (204), `status` is the string status the body claims in subsonic-response. These are two different quantities with the same name in one 248-line file, and Check passes one where the other is in scope (145). | Rename the string parameter to `claimed` in collect and pickBranches, to match claimedStatus, so `status` always means the HTTP code. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/conformance/conformance.go:239` | medium | pointer and pointerEscaper (239-248) encode kin-openapi's []string path as an RFC 6901 string. The only caller decodes it again straight away (grade.go:488-497 pointerPath and pointerUnescape) and joins the segments with "/". That join brings back the ambiguity the escaping was there to remove. Two packages each hand-roll half of RFC 6901 to move a []string across one boundary. | Choose one form. Either expose the segments (`Path []string // JSON pointer segments, unescaped`) and delete both escapers, or keep Pointer for the published module and have grade split it with no rejoin. The first is the smaller change. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/conformance/conformance.go:75` | low | New names its parameter `document` and the loaded value `doc`, two names for the same idea, and its doc sentence reads "loads the OpenAPI document in document". pointer (241) is a bare noun used as a function name and has no doc, while the var it uses does.
