Violation.Reason (59) has no doc, while Pointer has one. The XML sibling calls the same field Message. | `func New(spec []byte)` with `doc, err := ...LoadFromData(spec)`. Rename pointer to jsonPointer, or drop it under finding 4. Give Reason a one-line doc, and use one word for that field in both tiers. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/conformance/conformance.go:153` | low | invalid is variadic (156), but all three of its callers pass exactly one Violation. The one site that has a slice (153) builds Result{Outcome: Invalid, Violations: out} by hand instead of calling it. | `return invalid(out...)` at 153, so the variadic form earns its place. Otherwise make invalid take a single Violation. |
| `internal/opensubsonic/conformance/conformance.go:181` | low | collect fills its result through an `out *[]Violation` pointer to a slice, with `*out = append(*out, ...)` at 195 and 197. The standard library writes an accumulator in append style. | `func appendViolations(dst []Violation, err error, claimed string) []Violation`, returning dst, as strconv.AppendInt and fmt.Appendf do. Check then writes `out := appendViolations(nil, err, claimedStatus(decoded))`. |

### internal/ops

Proud: no. Verdict: This is a small, well-bounded vocabulary package. It passes the actor as a method parameter instead of a request field, gives Privilege an invalid zero value, and makes WithDetail and WithRejects return copies, all of which show real judgment. The error vocabulary costs it the most: nine failures are spelled four times by hand (Code constants, sentinels, the codes map, and nine constructors), and production code matches almost none of the sentinels. The package doc promises an errors.Is contract that its only real consumer, opshttp, does not use.

12 findings: 1 high, 5 medium, 6 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (2), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/ops/errors.go:35` | high | (P5) Each of the nine failures is declared four times and kept in agreement by hand: Code constants (errors.go:16-31), sentinel vars (35-46), the codes map that joins them (51-62), and nine constructors that differ only in the sentinel (103-138). Production matches only two sentinels with errors.Is, ErrUnavailable and ErrConflict, both in cmd/dubplate-server/backup.go:241-243. ErrNotFound, ErrInvalidRequest, ErrForbidden, ErrCanceled, ErrInternal, ErrUnauthenticated, and ErrReauthenticationRequired have no production reader at all. The real consumer, opshttp/opshttp.go:203, classifies by ops.CodeOf, so Code is already the identity and the sentinel is a second name for it. The doc contract at doc.go:17-21 ('match with errors.Is against a sentinel, never by inspecting fields') describes a usage production does not follow: opshttp.go:220-221 reads Reason, Detail, and Rejects directly. An agent adding a tenth code will copy all four lists. BACKLOG #121 already tracks 'Code as the errors.Is target'. | Make Code the target and delete the sentinels and the map: `func (c Code) Error() string { return string(c) }`, `func (e *Error) Unwrap() error { return e.Code }` (or `Is(target error) bool { c, ok := target.(Code); return ok && c == e.Code }`), then `errors.Is(err, ops.CodeConflict)`. Collapse the constructors into one `func NewError(code Code, reason string) *Error`, with the empty-reason check for CodeUnavailable inside it. Codes() becomes a literal slice next to the constants, and doc.go:17-21 should describe the contract opshttp actually uses. |
| `internal/ops/admin.go:67` | medium | One concept has two wire names: a setting is `name` in SettingEntry.Name (admin.go:67) and `key` in SetSettingRequest.Key, ResetSettingRequest.Key, and the two responses (admin.go:85, 92, 99, 105). Ids are just as uneven: a job id is the typed Handle (ops.go:99), but Member.UserID (admin.go:9), SubmissionID (submissions.go:52), and FileID are plain strings. The job record also calls its id ID (ops.go:136) while every request calls it JobID. Detail is an untyped string (details.go:6, errors.go:90, :142) while its sibling Code is typed. These are wire contracts, so each inconsistency hardens with every client. | Pick one name per concept and use it everywhere: SettingEntry.Key with `json:"key"`, or Name in every request. Declare `type Detail string` for the Detail constants, the Error.Detail field, and WithDetail's parameter. Either type every wire id the way Handle is typed or keep them all plain strings; do not mix. |
| `internal/ops/privilege.go:69` | medium | (P5, P8) The twelve operation names live in parallel hand-kept lists: the untyped Op constants (admin.go:111-119, submissions.go:10-16), the privileges map (privilege.go:69-83), and opshttp's route table (opshttp/opshttp.go:98-110). The map also restates seam membership, since every AdminSeam operation maps to PrivilegeAdmin and every SubmissionSeam operation to PrivilegeMember. The names are untyped strings, so PrivilegeOf(op string) at :89 takes any string, while the package's other enums (Code, Privilege, JobState) are typed. The counts in comments ('seven' admin.go:110 and :121, 'five' submissions.go:9 and :137, 'twelve' privilege.go:66, 'thirteen' details.go:3, 'six' ops.go:111) are a further list that goes stale silently. | Declare `type Op string` and type the constants, so the table reads `var privileges = map[Op]Privilege{...}` and the call reads `PrivilegeOf(op Op)`. Drop the numerals from the comments. The constants then follow the type-prefix convention that Code and Privilege already use, which also answers the ops.Op near-stutter. |
| `internal/ops/admin.go:104` | low | Identical shapes are declared twice in two places: ResetSettingResponse repeats SetSettingResponse field for field (admin.go:91-108), and CancelJobResponse repeats JobStatusResponse (ops.go:164-177). submissions.go:125-135 solves the same problem with defined types (`type RetrySubmissionResponse GetSubmissionResponse`). The package handles one situation in two ways. | Use the submissions form throughout: `type ResetSettingResponse SetSettingResponse` and `type CancelJobResponse JobStatusResponse`. |
| `internal/ops/admin.go:126` | low | AdminSeam and SubmissionSeam name the architectural role ('seam' is test-seam jargon) instead of what the interface does. Stdlib interfaces are named for their behavior (fs.FS, http.Handler). At call sites they read as ops.AdminSeam, where ops.Admin says the same thing. | Use ops.Admin and ops.Submissions, and say 'the two interfaces' in doc.go:2. |
| `internal/ops/ops.go:54` | low | NetworkActor's parameter `id` shadows the imported package `id` (ops.go:8). It compiles only because the function body does not use the package. The client address has three names: the field `client` (:29), the parameter `clientAddr` (:54), and the method ClientAddress (:88). | Rename the parameter `ident` or `who`. Name the field `clientAddr` to match the parameter, or rename both to the method's word. |
| `internal/ops/ops.go:104` | low | ParseHandle calls id.Parse, discards the result, and returns the input string. Its only production caller, local/jobs.go:373-376, then calls id.Parse on the same string again. handleLen (:90-94) restates a length that internal/id, already imported, owns. Its comment says 'internal/ops imports no store type, so the length is restated here', which no longer holds once id is the source. BACKLOG #181 already tracks this. | Have ParseHandle return what was parsed, `func ParseHandle(s string) (id.ID, error)`, or give Handle an `ID() (id.ID, error)` method, so parseID parses once. Build the message from `2*len(id.ID{})` and delete handleLen with its comment. |

### internal/ops/admin

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. This is a thin, well-bounded implementation of ops.AdminSeam: files split by concern, every export has a production caller in cmd/dubplate-server, comments carry real reasons, and the context handling around the post-commit notice is careful. What costs it most is the test-only seam: Config.Window is filled in production only with the package's own exported ReauthWindow(acc), and the same pattern is already copied in ops/local and intake. After that come a hand-kept parallel wire vocabulary and member writes that copy their gate into every method instead of folding it into the write helper.

10 findings: 1 high, 2 medium, 7 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/ops/admin/members.go:45` | medium | RemoveMember (45-58) and DemoteMember (60-73) are the same body except for the roster method they call, and AddMember (29-31) repeats the same requireFresh call. The package doc promises that every member write is gated, but nothing enforces it: each method has to remember the call, and writeMember (75) only adds a timeout and refusal mapping. writeSetting (settings.go:130) folds the gate into its helper, so the two families gate in different places. | Make the member helper own the gate, like writeSetting does: `func (s *Seam) writeMember(ctx context.Context, actor ops.Actor, write func(context.Context, auth.Actor) error) error { if err := s.requireFresh(ctx, actor); err != nil { return err }; ...timeout, write(ctx, rosterActor(actor)), refusal }`. Then RemoveMember becomes parse, then `return ops.RemoveMemberResponse{}, s.writeMember(ctx, actor, func(ctx context.Context, by auth.Actor) error { return s.cfg.Roster.RemoveMember(ctx, id, by, s.cfg.Logger) })`. Parsing still runs before the gate. |
| `internal/ops/admin/settings.go:16` | medium | (P5) The wire values of ops.SettingEntry's Home, Apply, and Mark fields are declared in the implementation (homeStore, homeEnvironment, applyNames, markNames). The rest of the wire contract lives in ops: the Op* names (internal/ops/admin.go) and the Detail* values (internal/ops/details.go). applyNames and markNames also restate config.ApplyMode.String and config.Sensitivity.String (internal/config/settings.go:33, keys.go:28) with underscores, kept in step by hand. A new ApplyMode makes the map lookup return "", so the wire silently carries an empty apply mode. | Declare the values next to SettingEntry in ops, the way Detail* is declared (`const ( HomeStore = "store"; HomeEnvironment = "environment"; ApplyImmediately = "immediately"; ApplyNextOperation = "next_operation"; ... )`). Convert with a switch in admin that has an explicit default (a panic for a mode no one mapped, matching config's own zero-value-panics precedent), not a map that returns "". |
| `internal/ops/admin/admin.go:64` | low | The Seam field `settings map[string]config.Setting` has the same name as the imported package `settings`. Methods that use both read ambiguously: settings.go:133 has `s.settings[key]` and settings.go:136 has `settings.Author`. The map also keeps whole Setting values when only Mark is ever read. | `marks map[string]config.Sensitivity`, filled in New with `marks[s.Key] = s.Mark`, and read as `if s.marks[key] == config.Sensitive`. |
| `internal/ops/admin/config.go:58` | low | The local `refusal := fmt.Sprintf(...)` inside reauthWindowBelowCap.Check shadows the package-level function refusal (admin.go:118), which maps errors to wire answers. One identifier means two unrelated things in one package. | Rename the local to `msg` or `problem`. |
| `internal/ops/admin/notice.go:26` | low | The method `notice` reads at its call site (settings.go:150, `s.notice(ctx, actor, key, res)`) as the verb "to notice", meaning to observe, while it actually sends mail. The doc comments follow the misreading with "noticed" (settings.go:107, 119). The local `log` (line 29) breaks the `logger` naming used for *slog.Logger everywhere else in the package (Config.Logger). | `notifyAdmins` (with noticeText becoming `notification` or staying as is), "notified" in the doc comments, and `logger := s.cfg.Logger.With(...)`. |
| `internal/ops/admin/notice.go:38` | low | The principal lookup scans the whole roster and keeps assigning after the match, with no break. A reader has to check whether a later match is meant to win. | `if i := slices.IndexFunc(members, func(m auth.Member) bool { return m.ID == actor.UserID() }); i >= 0 { principal = members[i].Username }`. |
| `internal/ops/admin/settings.go:142` | low | writeSetting repeats writeMember's core: `context.WithTimeout(ctx, writeWait)`, defer cancel, run the write, `refusal(err)`. That is two copies of the one place that turns a store write into a bounded, mapped call. | One unexported helper, `func bounded(ctx context.Context, write func(context.Context) error) error`, that both writeSetting (capturing res in the closure) and the gated member helper call. |

### internal/ops/local

Proud: no. Verdict: The concurrency and recovery mechanism is careful and correct: every state change is guarded by the expected state in its WHERE clause, a track that closes the Add/Wait race, a WithoutCancel context for terminal writes, and a recovery that clears its own stranded records. The cost is on axis 4, which also shapes the exported surface. Config carries four reader closures that production always fills from the same accessor, through two exported constructors that exist only to build them, and startBackup and newJob each take a parameter only tests vary. The density of 0.30 is under database/sql's, but the comments sit in the wrong places: rationale is repeated three times and two comments cite the process that produced the code.

12 findings: 1 high, 5 medium, 6 low. Held by patterns: P1 (3), P2 (1), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/ops/local/jobs.go:41` | medium | The package uses five names for one concept. The type is `binder`; backupBind (local.go:170) returns a binder; backupJob (jobs.go:41) also returns a binder, though its name promises a job; the parameter is bindFor (local.go:149); and the argument is bind (local.go:195). backupBind and backupJob also sit in different files, and backupJob has one caller. | Fold backupJob into one `func (r *Runner) backupBinder(ctx context.Context) (binder, error)` that reads the three values and returns the closure. It belongs in the file that owns the backup binding. |
| `internal/ops/local/jobs.go:150` | medium | markRunning (150-176) and finish (207-234) are the same body with a different UPDATE. Each opens a storeContext, runs a conditional UPDATE guarded by the expected state, calls store.Affected, falls back to `SELECT state FROM jobs WHERE id = ?`, and logs a refusal. That SELECT appears a third time in requestCancel (296). The guarded transition is the package's central invariant (doc.go:11-14), and it is written out twice. | Make one implementation: `func (r *Runner) transition(j *job, query string, args ...any) (moved bool, state string, err error)` that owns the context, the Affected check, and the fallback read. markRunning and finish then call it and keep only their own log line. |
| `internal/ops/local/recovery.go:51` | medium | liveOwners (51-68) and recoverOwn's read loop (76-90) both scan one column of queued-or-running records into a slice, and they disagree on cleanup. recoverOwn joins rows.Close on a Scan error (84). liveOwners returns at 59-61 without closing the rows. The 'mark interrupted' UPDATE is also written twice (41-42 by owner, 96-97 by id). Separately, `context.WithTimeout(ctx, writeWait)` is repeated in conflict, insert, requestCancel, and recoverJobs beside storeContext, and conflict (local.go:268) wraps a context that recoverJobs then wraps again. | Write one scanner, for example `func column[T any](rows *store.Rows) ([]T, error)`, that always closes the rows, and call it from both. recoverOwn can collect the untracked ids and then call the same interrupt statement recoverJobs uses, with `WHERE id IN (...)` or one helper that takes the predicate. Add a `writeContext(ctx)` sibling to storeContext. |
| `internal/ops/local/recovery.go:51` | medium | 'Live' means two things in this package. doc.go:7 uses 'a live owner' for an owner whose lease is held, as opposed to a dead one. liveOwners returns the owners of queued or running records, which is exactly the set that may include dead owners, and recoverJobs then tests each one for liveness. conflict (local.go:284) and the StartBackup doc (local.go:137) use 'live' for an unfinished record. A reader of recoverJobs reads `owners, err := liveOwners(...)` as already filtered. | Keep 'live' for one meaning, the lease being held, as doc.go defines it. Rename the helper to describe the records it reads: `unfinishedOwners` or `activeOwners`. Say 'unfinished job' at local.go:284 and in the StartBackup doc. |
| `internal/ops/local/config.go:36` | low | JobRetention returns a reader closure, but at the call site `local.JobRetention(acc)` reads as a value, the retention of acc. BackupConfigFrom uses a 'From' suffix where Go writes NewX. Both go away under the first finding. If either survives, its name should say what it returns. | Delete both through the accessor-in-Config fix. Otherwise use `RetentionReader(acc)` and `NewBackupConfig(cfg, acc)`. |
| `internal/ops/local/jobs.go:99` | low | jobs.go imports the package `id` and then shadows it with `id := j.id` (99) and with `id []byte` parameters on requestCancel (290), status (325), and readStatus (338). The job also stores two representations of one value, `id []byte` and `handle ops.Handle` (68-69). handleOf (383) is hex.EncodeToString, which duplicates id.ID.String (internal/id/id.go:42), and id.ID already implements driver.Valuer (id.go:49). | Store `id id.ID` on job and derive the handle with `ops.Handle(j.id.String())`. Pass id.ID straight to SQL, and name parameters `jobID` so the package name stays visible. parseID's double parse (372-381) is already tracked as BACKLOG #181. |

### internal/ops/opshttp

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The wire mechanism is excellent: strict decoding, a deliberate disclosure policy for errors, and denial causes that are safe to log. But admission runs through an authorizer interface, a context round trip, and a channel parameter that exist only so tests can inject a fake, and the package doc still says nothing is exported. Smaller issues: a duplicated unauthenticated sentinel, a route table hand-kept in step with ops.privileges, and a misnamed authorizingSeam.

10 findings: 1 high, 5 medium, 4 low. Held by patterns: P1 (2), P3 (1), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/ops/opshttp/authorize.go:29` | medium | authorizingSeam is not a seam. In this package "seam" means ops.AdminSeam and ops.SubmissionSeam, and this type wraps neither: it has one method, admit, and its own constructor doc calls it "the admission gate" (authorize.go:34). As a result, h.seam.admit at opshttp.go:122 reads as a call into the ops seam. | Name it for what it does, for example `type gate struct` with `newGate` and the field `h.gate`, or fold it away under the first finding. |
| `internal/ops/opshttp/mount.go:16` | medium | (P6) opshttp.ErrUnauthenticated restates ops.ErrUnauthenticated (internal/ops/errors.go:44), which already maps to CodeUnauthenticated and which nothing in production raises today. To use its own sentinel, the mount hand-maps the error at mount.go:68-76 instead of going through respond and the statuses table. intake.ErrUnauthenticated (internal/intake/tuswrap.go:21) is a third copy with the same meaning. Three sentinels for one fact is the pattern the next mount will copy. | Have the Verifier wrap ops.ErrUnauthenticated (or return ops.NewUnauthenticatedError), drop the opshttp export, and match it with errors.Is(err, ops.ErrUnauthenticated). The response then derives from statuses like every other code. Keep the mount's logging rule (the verifier logs its own failures) in its one switch arm. |
| `internal/ops/opshttp/opshttp.go:97` | medium | (P5) The route map repeats each op name twice per entry (key and route argument). It is also a second hand-kept list of the twelve names in ops.privileges (internal/ops/privilege.go:69). A privilege with no route goes unnoticed. A route with no privilege only fails at request time, as not_found from admit. checkStatuses guards the other parallel table at construction, but nothing guards this one. The handler doc at opshttp.go:65 also hard-codes "twelve", a count that will go stale. | Register through one generic helper that writes the key once, `func handle[Req, Resp any](h *handler, op string, call func(context.Context, ops.Actor, Req) (Resp, error))`, which sets h.routes[op]. Add one test asserting that maps.Keys(h.routes) equals the set ops.PrivilegeOf accepts. Drop the number from the doc comment. |
| `internal/ops/opshttp/opshttp.go:138` | low | Several small naming inconsistencies. The local `route` in ServeHTTP shadows the package-level generic func route. mount's field `ops` (mount.go:31) reads against the imported ops package in the same method (m.ops.ServeHTTP beside ops.CodeNotFound). The type `admitted` (carrier.go:17) uses a participle as a noun, while errNoCaller (carrier.go:30) and its own doc say "caller". errorDetail has a Detail field (opshttp.go:239). Body limits use two vocabularies, maxBody and bodyCap/bodyCaps. | Rename as follows: `fn, ok := h.routes[name]`, `mount.handler` (or `next`), `type caller struct`, `wireError` for the inner struct, and `defaultBodyCap` beside bodyCaps. |
| `internal/ops/opshttp/opshttp.go:247` | low | writeError takes both a status and a code, even though statuses already relates the two, so callers pick one of two forms. Some pass a literal status (opshttp.go:140,145,149,154 and mount.go:64). Others look it up, as in statuses[ops.CodeInternal] at opshttp.go:208,229 and mount.go:74, or statuses[ops.CodeUnauthenticated] at mount.go:70. The next caller will copy whichever form it sees first. | `func writeError(w http.ResponseWriter, code ops.Code, reason string)` derives the status from statuses. Only the 405 arm, the one place the status departs from the code, calls writeErrorBody with an explicit http.StatusMethodNotAllowed. |

### internal/reject

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The core is a clean, net/http-status-style table: typed codes, one ordered slice, a clone-returning All, and a Lookup/Of split that separates storage text from programmer error. It falls short of the bar because three exported fields (Blocking, Param, Canonical) have no production reader, and because the one parameterized reason pushes its special case out to every caller, which has already been copied twice and missed once.

9 findings: 2 high, 2 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P2 (3), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/reject/reject.go:190` | medium | (P5) bad-extension's wording is assembled in three places that must agree by hand: the table entry (:71-75), the badExtensionWording format (:68), and WithFormats' body, which carries the non-FLAC Label and NextStep as literals (:195-197). Canonical repeats the Sprintf but fills only Friendly. A 'canonical' bad-extension would therefore pair the generic sentence with the label "not FLAC" and the FLAC next step, a mix no page should render. joinFormats also returns "" for an empty slice (:209-210), so WithFormats(nil) renders "...the library keeps ()." narrow (internal/intake/limits.go:215) can produce an empty allowlist when a collection's list is disjoint from the bound. Finally, `strings.Join(names, "")` as the one-name case hides a plain `names[0]`. | Make WithFormats (or its renamed successor) the only implementation of the parameterized wording. Keep the non-FLAC label, friendly and next step together as one `Reason` literal beside the table entry rather than inside the function body. Any canonical form should be that function's output, not a second Sprintf. Decide what an empty format list means, either a panic as a programming error or the FLAC strings, and write `case 1: return names[0]`. |
| `internal/reject/reject.go:70` | low | `set` names an ordered slice whose order All's doc makes load-bearing ("adopted reasons first"), so the name says the opposite of the property that matters. Separately, record 0015 calls the wording `friendly_reason`, while the wire tag is `friendly` (:59). That leaves the record and the code with two names for one contract. | Rename `set` to `reasons`. Correct 0015 to the wire name `friendly`, since the JSON is the contract. |
| `internal/reject/reject.go:179` | low | Of panics on an unknown code, but its name gives no sign of it. The stdlib marks panicking twins with Must (regexp.MustCompile, template.Must). At the 19 call sites, `reject.Of(reject.TooLarge)` also names the package twice. | Either `MustLookup`, or a method on the code type, `func (c Code) Reason() Reason`, which reads `reject.TooLarge.Reason()` at the call site and keeps the panic documented on the method. |
| `internal/reject/reject.go:190` | low | In Go, a `With` prefix reads as a functional option or a derived value (context.WithCancel, slog.Logger.With). `reject.WithFormats(p.Allowlist)` at gate/manifest.go:122 does not say that it returns the bad-extension reason. | Name the reason it returns, for example `reject.BadExtensionFor(formats)`. Better, fold it into the context-taking entry point from the boundary finding. |
| `internal/reject/reject_test.go:99` | low | (P9) TestSetHoldsTwentySixBlockingAndThreeWarnings is a change detector that every new reason must edit. It adds nothing over the `warnings` map check at :81. TestNewReasonsNamedBySpec (:113) is named after the process that produced it. The comments at :10 and :54-55 cite "PCF", "the salvage package", "W8.2's catalogue" and "conductor ruling 6", process references a reader of the package cannot resolve. | Delete the count test. Rename the spec test for the property it checks, for example TestDraftedReasonsBlock. Replace the citations with the rule itself: "words a member-facing reason must not use". |

### internal/serve

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The security mechanisms are excellent: the trailer scrub, the rightmost-untrusted hop scan, the tightened CrossOriginProtection, and a shutdown that cannot leave a half-dead listener. But the package exports about twenty identifiers that no other package calls, passes addresses around as strings that four or five places parse again, and has comment density 0.87 against httputil's 0.45, with spec and task citations throughout.

17 findings: 3 high, 9 medium, 5 low. Held by patterns: P1 (4), P2 (3), P3 (1), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/serve/bindaddrs.go:46` | high | Two values are parsed and then passed on as strings, so the callers parse them again. SplitBindAddrs validates with net.ParseIP and returns []string. doctor.go:102 and doctor.go:220 then parse each entry again with netip.ParseAddr, and each copy carries a comment about 'a disagreement between the two parsers'. probe.go:83 parses them a third time. PeerAddress (clientaddr.go:137) returns a string that serve.go:187, probe.go:93 and clientaddr.go:198 each parse and unmap again with the same three lines. A comment that apologizes for two parsers disagreeing is a sign the type at the boundary is wrong. | Parse once with netip and pass the typed value: `func splitBindAddrs(raw string) ([]netip.Addr, error)`, with listen dialing `netip.AddrPortFrom(a, uint16(port)).String()`, and `func peerAddr(r *http.Request) (netip.Addr, bool)` returning the unmapped address. The disagreement branches and their comments then go away. |
| `internal/serve/clientaddr.go:109` | medium | Names are inconsistent across the package. The ProxyMode constants are ModeDirect and ModeTrustedProxy, so the type's name is not their prefix (stdlib style is http.SameSiteLaxMode under SameSite, or ProxyDirect and ProxyTrusted). PeerAddress and ClientIP return the same kind of value under two nouns. SplitBindAddrs and ParseProxyAddrs perform the same comma-list address parse under two verbs and two error shapes. group3KeepSet (strip.go:189) puts the spec's numbering in an identifier. identityExtras (strip.go:102) is the whole identity set, not extras to anything. terminated() (serve.go:246) reads like a predicate but is the dispatcher. The middleware shapes differ for no reason: Normalize(next), securityHeaders(hsts, next), refuseEchoMethods(next), Strip.Wrap(next), originCheck.wrap(next). There is no stutter against the package name. | Rename: proxyDirect and proxyTrusted (or mode with modeDirect and modeTrusted), peerAddr and clientAddr, parseBindAddrs to match parseProxyAddrs, hopByHopExempt, identityHeaders, dispatch. Give every chain step one shape, `func(next http.Handler) http.Handler`, as unexported functions or methods. |
| `internal/serve/clientaddr.go:261` | medium | splitHops and trimHop rebuild the stdlib by hand. Every caller skips an empty token, so splitHops behaves exactly like strings.Split (or strings.SplitSeq), and trimHop is strings.Trim(hop, " \t"). In the same file, CheckProxyHeader (clientaddr.go:61) writes out foldedKey's body inline, and sameHeaderName (strip.go:337) is a third way of comparing canonical names. | Use `for raw := range strings.SplitSeq(v, ",") { hop := strings.Trim(raw, " \t") ... }`, delete splitHops, trimHop and isHopSpace, and call foldedKey(name) in CheckProxyHeader. |
| `internal/serve/doctor.go:252` | medium | trustedProxy calls ResolveTrustedProxy and then calls ParseProxyAddrs on the raw config value again (doctor.go:261) to rebuild the proxy set the TrustedProxyConfig already holds. ClientIPRowStatus likewise reads the header back out of cfg (doctor.go:55) instead of using the resolved value. doctor.go is in the same package, so proxy.proxies and proxy.header are directly visible. | Delete trustedProxy. Use `proxy, err := ResolveTrustedProxy(cfg)`, then `proxy.proxies` for the set and `proxy.header` for the header. |
| `internal/serve/prefix.go:84` | medium | validatePrefix's segment loop (prefix.go:84-96) repeats pathRefusal's loop (normalize.go:114-124): trim the leading slash, split on '/', refuse '.' and '..', and refuse an empty segment anywhere but last. NewPrefix's doc says the point is to refuse exactly what Normalize refuses, so the two copies have to be kept in agreement by hand. The backslash check is duplicated the same way (prefix.go:59, normalize.go:105). | Make one helper the implementation, for example `func segmentFault(path string) (dot, doubled bool)`, called by both pathRefusal and validatePrefix. Each caller then maps the result to its own status code or message. |
| `internal/serve/clientaddr.go:70` | low | refusedProxyHeaders rebuilds its map on every call. Four of its literal keys (Forwarded and the three X-Forwarded-Host, -Proto and -Prefix) repeat members of forwardedAddressHeaders by hand, so the two lists must be kept in agreement manually. | Build the map once as a package-level var. Either give each non-address forwarded header its reason next to its entry in forwardedAddressHeaders, or derive the refusal from that list. |
| `internal/serve/normalize.go:81` | low | The refusal pair `markRefusal(r, s); http.Error(w, http.StatusText(s), s)` is written out three times: normalize.go:81-82, serve.go:230-231, and origin.go:80-81. A step that refuses but forgets markRefusal silently drops out of the refusal log, and nothing ties the two calls together. | Add one helper, `func refuse(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request, status int) { markRefusal(r, status); http.Error(w, http.StatusText(status), status) }`, and call it at all three sites. |

### internal/settings

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The mechanism is strong: the generation-keyed Accessor cache, rows read inside the write's own transaction, and a per-key before/after refusal check. The surface around it is weaker: the vocabulary of where a value comes from is spelled three ways, the settings-row scan is copied from store, and Set, Reset, and write take nine positional parameters. DENSITY: 0.415 (280 comment lines over 675 code lines) against database/sql at 0.424 (1243/2934), with expvar at 0.220. The overall rate is at stdlib level, but the excess sits in keep.go at 0.597.

14 findings: 1 high, 7 medium, 6 low. Held by patterns: P2 (5), P3 (2), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/settings/evaluate.go:26` | high | (P6) selectRows, rowScanner, scanRows, readRows (evaluate.go:26-167) and readTxRows (apply.go:101) repeat store's readSettingsFacts (internal/store/inspect.go:463): the same column list, the same sql.NullString seed scan, and the same micro-stamp conversion. The settings copy uses time.UnixMicro(at).UTC() where store uses store.Micros. store owns SettingsRow, so two packages now keep one table's column list and decode in agreement by hand. An agent adding a column will update one of them. | store owns the scan of its own row type: one unexported scanSettingsRows in store, reached by (*Handle).SettingsRows(ctx) and (*Tx).SettingsRows(ctx) (or one exported func over a Next/Scan interface). Settings then deletes selectRows, rowScanner, scanRows, readRows, and readTxRows and calls h.SettingsRows / tx.SettingsRows. |
| `internal/settings/evaluate.go:37` | medium | At package level the seed-change constants Seed, Reseed, Remove, and Unchanged sit next to the functions Set and Reset, so settings.Set and settings.Reset are funcs while settings.Seed and settings.Reseed are constants of a near-rhyming family. settings.Remove reads as a function call at its call site (cmd/dubplate-server/doctorrows.go:81). The unexported `type change` (write.go:77, an admin row edit) also differs from the exported `type Change` (evaluate.go:33, a seed verdict) only by case, and the two mean different things. | Prefix the enumeration with its type, as slog.LevelInfo does: ChangeNone, ChangeSeed, ChangeReseed, ChangeRemove. Rename the unexported type to `edit`, which its own doc already calls it ("the one row edit a write decides on"). |
| `internal/settings/keep.go:115` | medium | The package finds a setting by key four ways: byKey (keep.go:115), lookup (write.go:173, which calls reg.Settings() twice and clones the slice both times), the ContainsFunc inside evaluate's row loop (evaluate.go:179, O(rows x settings)), and NewAccessor's map (accessor.go:35). byKey's "defensive" zero value makes LocalSnapshotKeep fail with an empty key in its message (`: default "": ...`), which is worse than lookup's ErrNotSetting. | lookup is the implementation. Delete byKey and call `lookup(reg, store.LocalSnapshotKeepSetting.Key)`. In evaluate, build `registered := make(map[string]bool)` once and test membership there. Better still, config.Registry grows `Setting(key) (Setting, bool)` and every one of these calls it. |
| `internal/settings/apply.go:118` | low | insertAudit takes four adjacent strings (kind, actor, channel, key), which are easy to transpose. Apply passes the literals "system" and "env-seed" (apply.go:87), restating an Author it never names. The audit kind vocabulary is split between Change.String (evaluate.go:48) and the literals "set" and "reset" (write.go:123,163). | Use `insertAudit(ctx, tx, at, kind string, by Author, key string, old, new any)` with `var seedAuthor = Author{Actor: "system", Channel: ...}` declared once. Keep the audit kinds in one const block. |
| `internal/settings/evaluate.go:248` | low | Error text is repeated: "stored %s value refused" at accessor.go:130 and evaluate.go:248,289, "stored row has unknown source" at evaluate.go:251 and write.go:157, and the reset command spelled inline at evaluate.go:276 as well as in resetHint at evaluate.go:306. Line 248 also packs five nested calls into one expression. | Add one helper per message, for example `storedRefusal(stored *Row, err error) error` and `unknownSource(key string, stored *Row) error`, and split line 248 into a named unparseable value before wrapping it. |
| `internal/settings/write.go:247` | low | commit does not commit: it writes the row edit, audit row, and generation bump inside a transaction that h.Update commits later. Inside a transaction body that name misleads. Also, `reg` names a config.Setting in Accessor.read (accessor.go:66), while every other `reg` in the package is a *config.Registry. Other names are vague or clumsy: refuseNewRefusals (write.go:224), describe (write.go:182), and ErrEnvironmentSets (write.go:27), a sentence fragment next to ErrNotSetting and ErrInvalidValue. | Rename commit to writeEdit, Accessor.read's `reg` to `registered`, refuseNewRefusals to checkNoNewRefusals, and ErrEnvironmentSets to ErrSetByEnvironment. |

### internal/store

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. The crash-safe mechanisms are written with real judgment: the serve lock-conversion loop, the Tx that remembers its first error, the savepoint trap for migrations that end their own transaction, and the resumable swap plan. What costs the package most is duplication: SQLite result codes are hand-numbered in four files, four opens each build a Handle and then fill in its fields, and refusal texts are copied word for word. Next comes the Inspect Facts surface, which pulls other packages' schemas into the store.

22 findings: 2 high, 10 medium, 10 low. Held by patterns: P1 (1), P2 (2), P3 (2), P4 (2).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/store/write.go:16` | high | SQLite primary result codes are hand-numbered in four files (open.go:30 sqliteNotADBCode, conn.go:29-32 busy/readonly, migrate.go:37 sqliteErrorCode, write.go:16-21 nomem/interrupt/ioerr/full). The same `errors.As(err, &se) && se.Code()&0xff == X` idiom is repeated at open.go:514, conn.go:231, migrate.go:382, write.go:235 and write.go:249. An agent adding a code will copy both the magic number and the mask. The driver already exports the constants (modernc.org/sqlite/lib: `const SQLITE_NOTADB = 26` at lib/sqlite.go:4078). | Give the mask one home and use the library's names: `import sqlite3 "modernc.org/sqlite/lib"` and `func primaryCode(err error) (int, bool) { var se *sqlite.Error; if !errors.As(err, &se) { return 0, false }; return se.Code() & 0xff, true }`, kept next to classifyErr in conn.go. Then `switch c, _ := primaryCode(err); c { case sqlite3.SQLITE_BUSY: ... }` everywhere, and delete the eight local constants. |
| `internal/store/config.go:116` | medium | BusyTimeout (116-128) and MigrationWait (133-145) are the same parse-then-range body. They differ in that the first returns rangeError wrapping ErrOutOfRange and the second returns a plain fmt.Errorf. A caller can therefore tell an out-of-range busy timeout from a parse failure but cannot do the same for the migration wait. Keeping two floor/ceiling forms for the busy timeout (MS consts at 33-36 and Duration vars at 42-45) is a parallel list that has to stay in agreement by hand. | Use one helper: `func boundedDuration(environ []string, th config.Threshold, floor, ceil time.Duration) (time.Duration, error)` that returns rangeError for either bound, with both exported functions as one-line calls. Render the ms text from the Duration (`floor.Milliseconds()`) so only the Duration bounds remain. |
| `internal/store/conn.go:36` | medium | Three 'busy' words mean three things. ErrBusy (conn.go:36, message "store busy") is SQLite's write lock outlasting the busy timeout. ErrLockBusy (lock.go:22) is the flock on store.lock. StateBusy (inspect.go:30) is reported for ErrLockBusy, not ErrBusy. A caller matching `store.ErrBusy` to mean 'the store is busy' (internal/ops/admin/admin.go:121, internal/intake/seam.go:130) is matching the less obvious one. | Name the SQLite sentinel for its cause, for example `ErrWriteBusy` with the message "store write lock busy", so ErrLockBusy and StateBusy stay paired and the bare 'busy' word is no longer ambiguous. |
| `internal/store/conn.go:80` | medium | Four places call openHandle and then fill in its fields by hand: open.go:180-186 (version, lock, leaf), open.go:306-311, open.go:355 with 366-367, and restore.go:527-532. A new mode will copy the field-poking, and a forgotten `h.leaf = l` gives a Handle whose leases and backup refs resolve under the zero leaf without any error. The field docs at conn.go:61 and conn.go:70 then have to describe a 'bare file' handle that only tests create (conn_test.go:69, write_test.go:56). | Make the constructor take what every production caller sets: `func openHandle(l leaf, version int, busyTimeout time.Duration) (*Handle, error)`. OpenWrite and openShared then set only `h.lock`. Tests that want a bare file pass `leaf{dbPath: path}` explicitly. |
| `internal/store/lease.go:91` | medium | TryLease returns true when someone else holds the lease, which is the opposite of the Try convention (sync.Mutex.TryLock returns true when it acquired). The only caller has to rename the result to make sense of it (internal/ops/local/recovery.go:33 `held, err := r.store.TryLease(owner)`). The name also omits that this is the instance lease, beside TakeInstanceLease and TakeKindLease. | Name it for what it reports: `func (h *Handle) InstanceLeaseHeld(instance string) (bool, error)`, or `InstanceLive`. The doc keeps the note that a dead lease's file is removed. |
| `internal/store/migrate.go:361` | medium | rollbackMigration (migrate.go:361-367) and Tx.rollback (write.go:135-141) are the same body. They differ only in which error they pass to endsTransaction (err there, tx.err here). They share one subtle rule about SQLite rolling back on its own, and that rule can now drift between the two copies. | Keep one: `func rollback(tx *sql.Tx, err, cause error) error` in write.go next to endsTransaction. Tx.rollback calls `rollback(tx.tx, err, tx.err)` and applyMigration calls `rollback(tx, err, err)`. |
| `internal/store/restore.go:712` | medium | (P5) The newer-store refusal is written five times: newerStore (open.go:640-642), migrate.go:115-116, restore.go:712 and restore.go:871 (identical text, both with 'resume with a binary at version %d or above'), and snapshot.go:170. The below-any-version text is repeated at open.go:528, migrate.go:113 and snapshot.go:172. Record 0015 makes refusal wording a contract, so copied text is a contract kept by hand. | Route every site through newerStore and one `belowAnyVersion(path string, v int64) error`, and add `newerRestored(path string, v, head int)` for the restore pair. restoredState and recheckVersion call the same function. |
| `internal/store/snapshot.go:50` | medium | takeSnapshot (50-95) and backupCopy (339-367) run the same five steps: openMaintenanceConn, checkFreeSpace, preCreate, copyInto, and remove-the-stage-on-failure. Each writes its own removal. The `var busy, walFrames, checkpointed int; QueryRowContext("PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)").Scan` block appears three times (snapshot.go:90-93, snapshot.go:386-388, swap.go:76-78). The dupl threshold misses all of it because each copy is short. | Extract `func stageCopy(ctx context.Context, db *sql.DB, staged string, bt time.Duration, want int) (err error)` that owns preCreate through remove-on-failure, and `func checkpointTruncate(ctx context.Context, q interface{ QueryRowContext(context.Context, string, ...any) *sql.Row }) (busy bool, frames, done int, err error)`. takeSnapshot adds the rename and syncDir, and backupCopy adds the no-wait connection. |
| `internal/store/doctor.go:53` | low | Report.RowStatus's switch (54-76) repeats the state words that State.String (inspect.go:54-75) already holds: "absent", "busy", "not a store", "refused", "older", "newer", "current". Only the suffixes differ. | Start from `status = r.State.String()` and add only the detail per state (`": " + r.Reason`, the version tuple, the holders). The String default ("unknown") then covers the unrecognized arm, and the comment at 72-74 can go. |
| `internal/store/inspect.go:382` | low | lastSuccesses, readCollectionRoots and readSettingsFacts are each the QueryAll loop written out by hand, with the same refusal text in three places (query, scan, rows.Err). The package exports QueryAll (query.go:12) but cannot use it because it is tied to *Handle. Four sites open the inspect DSN and defer its close by hand (open.go:498, inspect.go:321, snapshot.go:126, restore.go:494), while maintenance connections get openMaintenanceConn. | Have QueryAll take the read method it needs (`q interface{ QueryContext(context.Context, string, ...any) (*sql.Rows, error) }`) so *Handle and *sql.DB both satisfy it, and wrap the error once per reader. Add `openInspectConn(path, bt)` as the counterpart of openMaintenanceConn. |
| `internal/store/lock.go:120` | low | busyError (lock.go:120-126) and leaseBusyError (lease.go:231-241) are the same holder-naming refusal. The lease form says "acquired by" and argues that an inherited lock may outlive its acquirer. KindLease.DupFiles (lease.go:215) hands the store lock to a child in exactly the same way, so the store-lock form claims "held by pid" for the case the lease form calls wrong. | Use one `func holdersError(what, path string, sentinel error) error` with the "acquired by" wording for both locks. |
| `internal/store/open.go:120` | low | The reconciler's signature `func(ctx context.Context, h *Handle, stamp string, in RestoreIntent) ([]byte, error)` is written out at open.go:120 and open.go:375, and twice more in internal/backup/reconcile.go:109-110. | Declare `type Reconciler func(ctx context.Context, h *Handle, stamp string, in RestoreIntent) (report []byte, err error)` and use it in ServeOptions and finishRestore. backup.Reconciler then returns store.Reconciler. |
| `internal/store/open.go:419` | low | statLeaf loops over a slice of five paths and then switches on which path it is (425-430) to set hasLock and hasLeases, so the switch repeats the list. OpenServe (245-251) and OpenRestore (129-135) copy the FUSE warning log line verbatim. inspect.go:359 and snapshot.go:198 compute `st.Bavail*uint64(st.Frsize)` separately. | Stat lockPath and leasesDir by name and fold `complete` from those results and the other three. Let leaf.probe take the logger and log the warning itself. Add `freeBytes(dir string) (uint64, error)` for both statfs readers. |
| `internal/store/restore.go:180` | low | readRecord inlines leaf.readIntent (intent.go:157-170) to change the absent and unreadable refusals. The two decoders of one file can drift. | Have readIntent return `(*intentRecord, error)` with the decode error unwrapped and let each caller word its refusal, or give it a `whenUnreadable func(error) error` parameter. |
| `internal/store/stamp.go:22` | low | `store.Micros(us)` reads as a quantity of microseconds but returns a time.Time. RestoreStep's constants are StepSwap through StepServe (restore.go:748-759), while State and Standing prefix their constants with the full type name. DoctorRow returns four positional results, two of them bools (doctor.go:26), which are easy to swap at the one call site (cmd/dubplate-server/doctor.go:60). conn.go:57 refers to 'the write method' for what is Update. intentFilePerm (intent.go:24) also sets the report's mode. | Rename to `TimeFromMicros`, or `UnixMicroUTC` after time.UnixMicro. Rename the type to `Step`, or the constants to `RestoreStepSwap` and so on. Return the row as a struct `{Status string; Failed bool; Report Report; Checked bool}` or as `(Report, bool)` and let the caller call RowStatus. Change conn.go:57 to say [Handle.Update]. Use a neutral name such as `leafFilePerm`. |

### internal/webapp

Proud: no. Verdict: Workmanlike. The security mechanism is excellent: variants are built once at startup, every served variant's CSP is checked again, branding values are refused rather than escaped, and the operator CSS is read through an O_NOFOLLOW descriptor. The exported surface is what costs the package most. Five exports have no production caller outside the package, and three more exist only so cmd can rebuild an error or a doctor row that this package should own. document.go and webapp.go also read as grab bags rather than concerns.

21 findings: 6 high, 7 medium, 8 low. Held by patterns: P1 (2), P2 (7), P3 (1), P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/webapp/document.go:284` | medium | (P7) The package is named webapp, but its errors carry 'web: ' (28 sites) and its request record carries 'serve: document request', which is another package's prefix. The repo's convention is the package's own name (Errorf("auth: ...") in auth, "serve: ..." in serve). The branding and operator-CSS errors carry no prefix at all, because config.go wraps them with the key. An agent copying this file inherits a stale package name and starts logging under serve's name. | Use 'webapp: ' for errors and records, or name the package web and drop the prefix question. The record becomes `logger.Info("webapp: document request", ...)`. |
| `internal/webapp/webapp.go:191` | medium | (P5) The two favicons and their MIME types are listed twice: as the icon links documents carry (webapp.go:191-194) and as the files the icons route serves (static.go:108-111). The two lists must agree by hand, and BACKLOG #57 already cites both sites. | Keep one package-level table, `var favicons = []struct{ name func(RouteNames) string; contentType, sizes string }`, with New building the icon links and newIconsRoute building the files from it. |
| `internal/webapp/webapp.go:224` | medium | The documents table (webapp.go:131-138) does not say which Routes field each document lands in. New collects every handler into one map, then recovers members and server settings by rebuilding their paths and calling delete (lines 225-231). Then each Routes field is `exactPaths(group, notFound)` with no delete. |

### tools/close-evidence

Proud: yes. Verdict: Sound with nits. This is a small, well-shaped command: main stays thin over run(ctx, cfg, out), every subprocess goes through exec.CommandContext, and every fallible measurement finishes before any output is written. Most of the cost sits in run(), which measures and renders markdown in one 60-line body, and in a few loose names and representations. No boundary is wrong and nothing is exported.

9 findings: 0 high, 2 medium, 7 low. Held by patterns: P4 (1).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `tools/close-evidence/main.go:56` | medium | run collects five facts (lines 65-84) and then renders the whole markdown report inline (lines 86-117), in one function. The report is the tool's only contract, since it is pasted as printed. Because of this shape it cannot be built or tested without running git and go test, and the next section someone adds will grow the same body. An agent adding a report tool will copy this measure-and-print-in-one-function shape. | Split collection from rendering, the way cmd/go keeps its data separate from its text. Use `type evidence struct { revRange string; tests testRun; changedTests string; surfaces []surface; subjects, refactors []string }`, `func collect(ctx context.Context, cfg config) (evidence, error)`, and `func (e evidence) writeTo(w io.Writer) error`. run then becomes `e, err := collect(ctx, cfg); if err != nil { return err }; return e.writeTo(out)`. |
| `tools/close-evidence/main.go:129` | medium | Two durations use two types. testRun.wall is a time.Duration (line 122), but packageTime.elapsed is the raw string "0.412s". parsePackageTimes then checks the string with a hand-rolled `strings.HasSuffix(fields[2], "s")` (line 164). That accepts any token ending in s and keeps the value as text nobody can compare or sum. | Parse the token: `d, err := time.ParseDuration(fields[2]); if err != nil { continue }` and store `elapsed time.Duration`. The ParseDuration error is the check for "no duration reported", so the HasSuffix branch goes away, and the table renders both values the same way. |
| `tools/close-evidence/main.go:73` | low | (P5) The watched surfaces appear in three places that must agree by hand: the package doc (line 14), two changedPaths calls with their own variables (lines 73-80), and two render lines (lines 106-107). Adding a third surface means editing all three. | Use one table, `var surfaces = []string{"internal/serve", "go.mod"}`, loop over it once to collect and once to render, and word the doc comment generically ("the watched surfaces"). |
| `tools/close-evidence/main.go:90` | low | Seven calls pass constant strings with no verbs through fmt.Fprintf (lines 88, 90, 93, 95, 102, 105, 110), while lines 99 and 103 use b.WriteString for the same job. No linter in the gate flags this, and the mix is inconsistent within one function. | Use `b.WriteString("### Test wall time\n\n")` (or io.WriteString on the writer after the split above) for every constant, and keep Fprintf for formatted lines. |
| `tools/close-evidence/main.go:111` | low | The rendered line "match refactor or simplif" restates the alternation in refactorSubject (line 54) in prose, so a change to the regexp silently makes the report wrong about what it counted. | Render the pattern itself: `fmt.Fprintf(w, "%d of %d commits match %s.\n", len(refactors), len(subjects), refactorSubject)`. |
| `tools/close-evidence/main.go:123` | low | testRun.result is a string that holds either "pass" or "FAIL" (lines 143 and 150). That is a boolean stored as text, and the two values differ in case, so the rendered line reads `..., pass` in one run and `..., FAIL` in another. | Store `passed bool` and pick the word at render time, in one case convention (go test's own `ok`/`FAIL` if mirroring it is the intent). |
| `tools/close-evidence/main.go:141` | low | The local `run := testRun{...}` inside timeTests shadows the package-level func run. It compiles, but it is the kind of shadowing a reader has to stop for, and agents copy it. | Name it after its type, `tr := testRun{...}`, or return a composite literal directly. |
| `tools/close-evidence/main.go:176` | low | filterSubjects does not say what it filters for, so the call site at line 109 needs the variable name `refactors` to explain it. block (line 228) is also vague: it returns a fenced code block or a note. lines collects into `got` (line 217), which is test vocabulary in production code. | Use `refactorSubjects(subjects)`, `fenced(out, empty)`, and a plain accumulator name such as `out` or `ls`, as in the stdlib's strings and bufio helpers. |

### web

Proud: no. Verdict: Sound with nits. The embed mechanism is right: the `all:` prefix and a committed placeholder let a fresh checkout compile. But the one exported symbol puts the embed's directory layout in the API, and its doc comment justifies that with a claim that io/fs disproves. The test file adds a helper whose name and comment both misdescribe it, plus plan-ID citations a reader cannot resolve.

11 findings: 1 high, 4 medium, 6 low. Held by patterns: P2 (1), P3 (1), P4 (4).

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `web/embed.go:21` | medium | `web.Assets` is the whole build: documents, fonts, favicons, the mark, and `_app/`. In internal/webapp, `RouteNames.Assets` (webapp.go:37) and `Routes.Assets` (webapp.go:91) mean only the hashed `_app/` subtree. Both meanings meet in one file, cmd/dubplate-server/web.go, at line 32 (`Assets: assetsRouteName`), line 54 (`wr.Assets`), and line 103 (`web.Assets`). There one word names a part and the whole it belongs to. The consumer's own locals already say `embeddedBuild` and `buildFS`. | Rename it `web.Build`. That matches `npm run build`, adapter-static's build/ directory, and the consumer's names, and it leaves "Assets" meaning `_app/` everywhere. |
| `web/embed_test.go:65` | medium | `linkTagsWithHref` returns every `<link ` tag and never looks at href. The comment on stylesheetHrefAttrRE (lines 99-101) says the regex applies to "a tag [linkTagsWithHref] has already filtered to a rel=\"stylesheet\" one", but that helper does no filtering. The filtering happens in its two callers, each writing the same `strings.Contains(tag, `rel="stylesheet"`)` check by hand (lines 51-53 and 110-112). The name and the comment both misdescribe the code. | Rename it `linkTags`. Add a `stylesheetTags(body string) []string` that does the rel filter once, and have both TestNoFlashStaticHalf and stylesheetHrefs range over it. Then fix the comment on line 99 to say what is actually filtered. |
| `web/embed_test.go:91` | medium | The two DaisyUI declarations are written out as literals twice, at lines 91/94 and again at 160/163, each with its own error message. The doc on TestDaisyUIRootBackgroundRule (lines 81-84) says it exists "rather than a literal this file restates", and then the file restates the same literals. The two tests must stay in agreement by hand. | Hoist the declarations into one package-level `var rootBackgroundRule = []string{"background-color:var(--root-bg)", "--root-bg:var(--color-base-100)"}` and have both tests loop over it with one message shape. Drop the claim in lines 81-84. |
| `web/embed_test.go:173` | low | Test names mix two styles. `TestAssets_EmbedsHashedAssetDirectory` and `TestAssets_PlaceholderCommitted` use the `TestType_Method` underscore form, which is meant for methods, and Assets is a var. `TestNoFlashStaticHalf`, `TestDaisyUIRootBackgroundRule`, and `TestBuiltCSSCarriesDaisyUIRootBackgroundRule` are plain CamelCase. | Use one style. Since no method is under test, use `TestEmbedsHashedAssetDirectory` and `TestPlaceholderCommitted`. |
| `web/embed_test.go:312` | low | TestFontFaceSrcsResolveUnderFonts calls resolveStylesheetPath and does the `fonts/` CutPrefix twice per face, once in the set comparison (lines 295-298) and again in the subtest loop (lines 312-317). | Resolve once when each face is collected: add a `resolved` field to the `found` struct and read it in both loops. |

### web:svelte

Proud: no. Verdict: The runes are used correctly throughout. No effect mirrors state, no read follows an await, `$state.raw` is used on purpose, the module-level announcer is clean, and the client-init token capture is a good use of Kit 3's `ClientInit`. The app misses the bar at its seams: the two admin pages each copy the same hand-written page state machine with drift, data loads in `onMount` instead of a `load` function, and the lib/api layer has two transports with two result models while the API client also speaks UI-state and copy vocabulary. A third admin page written today would copy all of it.

23 findings: 1 high, 10 medium, 12 low.

| Site | Severity | Finding | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `web/src/routes/server-settings/+page.svelte:77` | high | Both admin pages hand-roll the same controller: `status`, `unreachableMessage`, `refreshFailed`, `notice`, `retry`, an in-flight flag, a `load`, a `refreshAfterWrite`, a `say`, and a refusal reader (`settle` in members, `refuse` here). Compare members/+page.svelte:85-172 with server-settings/+page.svelte:77-178. The copies have already drifted. `say` takes four positional arguments in members (148) and one `Notice` object here (149). The flag is `submitting` in one page and `busy` in the other, and the loader is `loadRoster` in one and `load` in the other. The field-error state is `erroredField` (members 103) in one and `notice.key` (settings 72) in the other. Each page also owns its signed-out redirect logic. This scores high because agents imitate the code around them: the next admin page will be a third copy. The dupl gate does not catch it, because the copies are reworded rather than byte-identical. | Extract the shared lifecycle into one reactive module, for example `lib/admin/admin-list.svelte.ts` exporting `class AdminList<T>` with `$state` fields `status`, `entries` (via `$state.raw`), `refreshFailed`, `notice` and `busy`. Give it `load()`, `refresh()`, and `write(op, request, { onOk })` methods, where `write` owns the signed-out, reauthenticate and retry handling. Each page then keeps only its field mapping and its copy. Classes with `$state` fields are the documented Svelte 5 pattern for shared reactive logic (svelte.dev/docs/svelte/$state#Classes). |
| `web/src/lib/api/post.ts:28` | medium | URL composition (`endpointPath`, plus the module-load check on `resolve('login')`) lives in the form-POST module, and v1.ts imports it from there (v1.ts:8). Meanwhile request.ts calls itself 'the one place the web app calls fetch' but leaves URL building to its callers. The two transports also read an answer into two different result models: post.ts uses `PostResult { ok, outcome: string, body }` with string-synthetic outcomes, and v1.ts uses the discriminated union `ApiResult`. Each re-derives the 403, non-JSON and network-failure cases on its own (post.ts:59-72, v1.ts:50-62). outcome.ts:1-5 claims to be 'the one outcome-to-text and outcome-to-class resolver', yet v1.ts:93-113 has a second one (`failureText`, `failureAlertClass`). | Move `endpointPath` (and its base-root derivation) into request.ts so `send(name, init)` takes the endpoint name. Make `postForm` return a discriminated union like `ApiResult`, for example `{ kind: 'answer'; ok; outcome; body } \| { kind: 'cross_origin' } \| { kind: 'network_failure' } \| { kind: 'failed' }`. Then one module reads the transport failures, and outcome.ts's header comment becomes true. |
| `web/src/lib/api/v1.ts:70` | medium | `loadList` is the transport client, but it answers in page-state vocabulary (`'refused' \| 'signedOut' \| 'unreachable'`) and returns a ready-made deck line (`message`). That makes lib/api depend on lib/copy (line 7) and on AdminPage's UI states. The union also duplicates by hand part of `Status`, which AdminPage.svelte:12 exports under a generic name. A new state added to one side will not be checked against the other. | Have `loadList` return `ApiResult<T[]>`, the same shape `call` already uses, and map it to a UI state in one place: the AdminPage controller from the high finding. If the union must stay in v1.ts, export it as `ListFailure` and write `type AdminPageStatus = 'loading' \| 'ok' \| ListFailure['state']` so a single source drives both. Rename `Status` to something specific. |
| `web/src/lib/components/AdminPage.svelte:36` | medium | `browser ? ... : ...` guards appear in six places: AdminPage.svelte:36, SiteHeader.svelte:39, +error.svelte:17, login/+page.svelte:27, and login/link/+page.svelte:30-31. The root +layout.ts sets `ssr = false`, so no component script ever runs on the server, including during prerender, which emits an empty shell. Every `browser` branch is dead code that tells the reader server rendering is possible when it is not. The idiom is repeated in every page, so agents will keep copying it. | Delete the guards and call `siteNameFromDocument(document)` and `takeLinkToken()` directly. Better, add a `servedSiteName()` helper to title.ts that reads `document` lazily, so the line each page copies today becomes one call. A module-level constant is not an option, because run.ts imports title.ts under Node. The Svelte guidance that effects and client code never run on the server applies here (svelte.dev/docs/kit/page-options#ssr). |
| `web/src/lib/components/SiteHeader.svelte:29` | medium | The header component owns the whole theme mechanism: the cookie name and its attributes, the `data-theme` stamp names, dark-mode detection, and restamping the `theme-color` meta (lines 29-72). That domain logic has nothing to do with rendering a navbar, and a second theme control (for example on the login page) would have to copy it or import it from a component. `dark` is also read once (line 46), so a device on `dubplate-system` that changes its OS preference while the page is open leaves the toggle out of step. | Move the mechanism to `lib/theme.ts`, exporting `currentlyDark()` and `choosePalette(palette)`, and keep the component as markup plus one handler. If the system-preference case matters, back `dark` with `MediaQuery` from `svelte/reactivity` (svelte.dev/docs/svelte/svelte-reactivity#MediaQuery). |
| `web/src/lib/components/SiteHeader.svelte:92` | medium | The component knows post.ts internals. It lists the synthetic outcomes by name (`'network_failure' \|\| 'cross_origin'`) and applies a trust rule: a non-2xx `signed_out` is not trusted, so it is mapped to `failed`. That rule reads the transport's result model and belongs in lib/api. Any other caller of `postForm` would have to rediscover it. | Once `postForm` returns a discriminated union (see the post.ts finding), the header becomes `if (r.kind === 'answer' && r.ok && r.outcome === 'signed_out') goto(...); else announce(outcomeText(r.kind === 'answer' ? 'failed' : r.kind), 'assertive')`. Better still, put a `signOut(): Promise<boolean>` in auth.ts beside `requestLink` and `consumeLink`. The header currently calls `postForm` directly while the other auth endpoints go through auth.ts, which is a boundary inconsistency of its own. |
| `web/src/lib/components/WriteNotice.svelte:13` | medium | The notice shape is declared three times: inline here at line 13, as `Notice` in members/+page.svelte:77, and as `Notice` in server-settings/+page.svelte:67. `retry` is modeled as separate nullable state (members 102, settings 87) that each page has to keep in step with `notice.reauthenticate` by hand. The prop type therefore allows `reauthenticate: true` with `retry: null`, which renders a 'Try again' button that does nothing (`onclick={() => void retry?.()}`, line 36). | Export one type from WriteNotice's `<script module>`, as AdminPage does with `Status`, and make the illegal state unrepresentable: `type Notice = { text; alertClass; reauthenticate: null } \| { text; alertClass; reauthenticate: { retry: () => Promise<void> } }`. Drop the separate `retry` prop and the separate `retry` state in both pages. |
| `web/src/lib/copy/deck.ts:48` | medium | The wire vocabularies are typed as plain `string`: `AuthResult.outcome` (auth.ts:19), `PostResult.outcome`, `Entry.home`, `apply`, `mark` and `source` (server-settings:48-57), and `call(operation: string, request: object)` (v1.ts:44). Every deck table is `Record<string, string>` (deck.ts:48, 154, 172, 249, 255, 262). A typo such as `result.outcome === 'signed_ot'`, `AUTH_OUTCOMES.faild`, or `call('list_member')` still compiles, even though openapi.yaml and `internal/auth/handlers.go` define closed sets. `TITLES` (line 332) already uses `as const`, so the deck is inconsistent with itself. | Declare the closed sets as unions next to the client, for example `type AuthOutcome = 'sent' \| 'requested' \| ...` and `type Operation = 'list_members' \| 'add_member' \| ...`. Type the deck tables as `{...} as const satisfies Record<AuthOutcome, string>`, which still uses only erasable syntax and so stays legal under Node's strip-only loader. The gate's deck-coverage check could then compare unions with unions instead of strings with strings. |
| `web/src/routes/members/+page.svelte:144` | medium | Both admin pages load their list in `onMount(() => { void loadRoster(); })`, here and at server-settings/+page.svelte:145. No route has a `+page.ts`. With the root's `ssr = false`, a universal `load` runs only in the browser, so the server-only ban does not rule it out. Kit's data path (`load`, `depends`, `invalidate`) is bypassed for a hand-built `'loading'` state and a hand-built refresh. The loaders also never use the `fetch` Kit passes in. As a side effect, app.html:10's `data-sveltekit-preload-data="hover"` preloads only code, because there is no data to preload. | Move the list read into `+page.ts`: `export const load = async ({ depends }) => { depends('app:members'); return { roster: await loadList(...) }; }`. Render from `data`. After a write call `invalidate('app:members')`, which keeps the old `data` on screen until the new data resolves and so covers the refresh-failure rule. If the visible loading state or hover-preloading a POST is deliberately unwanted, record that ruling in the AdminPage header comment so the next page does not have to rediscover it (svelte.dev/docs/kit/load, svelte.dev/docs/kit/page-options#ssr). |
| `web/src/routes/server-settings/+page.svelte:48` | medium | The `list_settings` wire type (`Entry`) and the `set_setting` answer type (`Written`, line 60) are declared inside the page, while the `list_members` wire type (`Member`) lives in lib/api/v1.ts:12. Wire contracts are scattered across two layers, and `Entry` is a generic name for what is a setting. | Move `Entry` (renamed `Setting`) and `Written` (for example `SettingWrite`) into v1.ts beside `Member`, so every openapi.yaml shape lives in the client module. |
| `web/src/routes/server-settings/+page.svelte:197` | medium | outcome.ts:48-50 states the codebase's own rule: table lookups go through `Object.hasOwn`, so that `toString` or `constructor` falls through. This page breaks it with `SETTING_APPLY[result.value.apply] ?? ''` (197, 233) and `SETTING_MARKS[e.mark] ?? e.mark` (257, 305), while `sourceLine` four lines below (237) follows it. A server-sent `apply` or `mark` of `constructor` would render a function's source text. The `Object.hasOwn(X, k) ? X[k] : fallback` pattern is also re-typed at seven sites (v1.ts:87-88, outcome.ts:31 and 56, members:169, settings:237). | Add one helper and use it everywhere: `export function lookup<V>(table: Readonly<Record<string, V>>, key: string, fallback: V): V { return Object.hasOwn(table, key) ? table[key] : fallback; }`. Put it in a small lib module, since deck.ts must stay import-free and so cannot host it. |
| `web/src/lib/api/auth.ts:6` | low | Imports between lib/api siblings use mixed styles. auth.ts imports `#lib/api/post.js`, while outcome.ts:7 (`./auth.js`), v1.ts:8-9 (`./post.js`, `./request.js`) and post.ts:8 (`./request.js`) use relative paths. | Choose one style for siblings (relative is conventional) and apply it across lib/api. |
